# 8. Decisions

Three kinds of decisions, kept apart so it is clear which ones are open:

- **Product**: taken by the project owner.
- **Architecture (approved)**: proposed during design and approved for 0.1.
- **Technical (open to challenge)**: taken during design or implementation, delegated.

## Product

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **The project is Rewind**, at `rewind.codes` (the owner, 13 November 2026) | It was ai-ptc. Identifiers say `rewind`: the crates `rewind-*`, the binary `rewind`, the script modules `"rewind"` and `rewind:tools`, the variables `REWIND_*`, the directory `.rewind/`, the key prefix `rewind_sk_`, the header `Rewind-Version`, the problem types `urn:rewind:problem:*`. The npm scope is `@rewind.codes`, the repository `planchon/rewind.codes`. Epoch 1 took the new names too (its modules and the labels its seeds are derived with), and its corpus was recorded again: no epoch had been released. |
| **The license is FSL-1.1-ALv2** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | The Functional Source License, the one Sentry uses: any purpose but a competing use, and each version becomes Apache 2.0 two years after its release. `LICENSE.md` at the root covers the repository; every crate and the SDK say `FSL-1.1-ALv2`, and the SDK carries a copy of the license for its npm package. |
| Rewind is a tool for **agent harness developers** | Server + TypeScript SDK + ready-made agent tools; the CLI is for local development. |
| **Durable workflows are the priority** | A short run is the same mechanism started with `wait`. |
| **Built to scale** | Compute apart from storage: runs in partitions on R2, Postgres for the control plane only, Turso for the embedded control plane (the owner, 22 November 2026; [rewrite](#taken-for-the-object-storage-rewrite-november-2026)). It was Postgres for everything. |
| **Payloads live on object storage**, except small ones | Three tiers by size: in the event, in the run's records in its partition, in a blob under the run ([storage](06-storage-and-scale.md#payloads)). |
| **Mocks are recorded from real runs** | Cassettes from journals; TypeScript mock handlers as a complement. |
| **Events can come from any source** | Harness, humans, other agents, other runs, external systems through webhooks. |
| **No `fetch`, no file system** | Network only through the opt-in, journaled `http` tool. No file system at all. |
| **The agent gets several tools**, not only `run_script` | `check_script`, `test_script`, `get_run`, `list_runs`, `signal_run`, `cancel_run`, `retry_run`, `search_tools`. |
| **Everything goes through the journal** | Replay re-executes against the journal, never restores a snapshot. |
| **Type-checking is in 0.1** | Generate → check → fix is the agent's main loop. |
| **`deno_core` + `deno_ast`** | Instead of raw `rusty_v8`. |
| **CLI surface for 0.1** | `run`, `check`, `test`, `replay`, `fork`, `trace`, plus `signal`, `cancel`, `server`. `rewind server` replaced `rewind worker` with the embedded mode (below). |
| **Concurrency, side effects, randomness**: handled as proposed | `seq` in emission order; tool policies and idempotency keys; seeded PRNG. |
| **OpenTelemetry for everything in the service** | Service telemetry and run telemetry, exported over OTLP. |
| **A dashboard of every run and all the code executed on the platform** | Built on the journal, not on an OTel backend. |
| **Every run has a TTL, 30 days by default** | `expires_at` in `run_started`; at the deadline the run is cancelled through the normal path. It bounds signal waits, tool calls that never answer, quarantine, and the life of a previous epoch. |
| **The system is measured by evals** | Models run end to end on the embedded engine against fake MCP servers. Choices that are cheap to change are decided on the numbers. |
| **A failed run can be forked with a corrected script** | `rewind fork` and `retry_run({ run_id, code })` as an agent tool. Fixing step 7 without redoing steps 1–6 is what the journal is for. |
| **Result types are inferred from the journal** when a server declares no `outputSchema` | Observed shapes, for type-checking and the prompt only. |
| **`run_script` returns a bounded result** | It lands in the model's context: output truncated above 256 KB, last log lines, errors with a code frame. |
| **What type-checks is what exists** | A baseline of deterministic web-standard globals; the checker's `lib` declares exactly the sandbox's globals; hints for absent ones such as `fetch`. |
| **A workflow starts and signals other workflows** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | A workflow is a script with a name and JSON Schemas for its input, its output and the signals it accepts. A script starts a run of a workflow, signals it, waits for its result, and signals an existing run of a workflow by its id; tsgo checks each of these calls ([workflows](01-product.md#workflows)). The harness registers workflows through the API, and the model through `save_workflow`. |
| **Runs reach each other through signals** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | Starting and signalling are built-ins the engine serves; the end of a run comes back to the run that started it as the reserved signal `rewind:ended:<id>`, rather than as an event of its own. The journal gets no new event, and cassettes already replay signals. Names starting with `rewind:` are reserved. |
| **A run started by a script is attached by default** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | Its parent does not end before it, and a parent that fails or is cancelled cancels it; `detached: true` starts an independent run. Rather than detached by default: a forgotten `result()` stays harmless, as a forgotten `await` is. |
| **A toolset pins workflows by version** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | The types a script was checked against hold for the run's whole life. A workflow therefore cannot start itself, nor form a cycle. Rather than the latest version at each start. |
| **A run of a workflow accepts only its declared signals** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | Validated whoever sends them, the API and webhooks included. Runs that are not a workflow's accept any signal, as before. |
| **A script reaches only the runs of its scope** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | The labels of the API key that started the first run of its tree, inherited by every run it starts. A workflow saved with a key limited to labels carries them. |
| **Epoch 1 takes the workflows** (the owner, 14 November 2026) | No epoch was released: the glue of epoch 1 gains `rewind:workflows`, and its corpus is recorded again. |

### Taken after the layering (November 2026)

Answers to the questions raised once the layering was done.

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **An invalid script fails fast** | Starting a run compiles, checks and type-checks the script (imports, names, default export, types) and refuses it before any run exists. The agent learns in the same call; it does not wait for an execution. |
| **An interrupted tool call is retried with its idempotency key** | `retry` is the default policy of every tool; `at_most_once` is an opt-out per tool in the toolset. After 5 attempts the call fails with `outcomeUnknown`. |
| **A toolset that changed during a run is reported, not failed silently** | The task is parked and the harness gets the webhook `tool.schema_changed`; the agent or the developer decides what happens to the run (closes question 3; [tools](04-tools.md#when-a-toolset-changes-during-a-run)). |
| **The cancellation grace is one delivery** | The script ends within the delivery of `cancel_requested`, or it is aborted (closes question 5). |
| **The CPU budget applies to each call into the script**, measured as CPU time | The watchdog reads the CPU clock of the isolate's thread, not wall-clock time. |
| **An activation that fails 5 times in a row quarantines the run** | With the last error as reason. The failure is logged and reported through OpenTelemetry and webhooks (closes question 16). |
| **Web-standard globals come from existing crates** | `deno_webidl`, `deno_url`, `deno_web` and the like, with their non-deterministic parts replaced (closes question 13). In general, take a maintained crate rather than rewrite one. |
| **A signal or a cancellation sent to a finished run is refused** | 409 in the API, an error in the CLI, instead of being dropped (closes question 4). |
| **The tenant lives in the run metadata**, never in the core | Closes question 7. |
| **Payload size and preview are blob metadata** | A `payloads (digest, bytes, preview)` table kept by the store (closes question 1). |
| **Webhooks: an unguessable URL and an HMAC signature** | Closes question 8. |
| **Run labels: a bounded map set at start**, exported to OpenTelemetry for allow-listed keys only | Closes question 10. |
| **TTL: a maximum per deployment** (365 days by default) | A run past its deadline ends `cancelled` with the reason `ttl`; no status of its own (closes question 15). |
| **No `LISTEN/NOTIFY`** | Wake-ups do not rely on Postgres notifications. |
| **The corpus lives in `rewind-e2e`** | A tooling crate allowed to depend on adapters. |
| **The embedded store is Turso Database** | The `turso` crate, the Rust rewrite of SQLite, rather than SQLite through rusqlite or libSQL (closes question 17). It is in beta and its multi-process access is experimental, so only the embedded server opens the database (below). The store stays behind `JournalStore` and its conformance suite, so the choice can change. |
| **`run_script` returns at most 256 KB of output** | Above it the output is truncated and the model pages through the rest (closes question 12). |
| **A quarantined run is released by hand, or retried when a new build is deployed** | A replay that fails again writes nothing, so the automatic retry is safe; a run nobody fixes ends at its TTL (closes question 14). |
| **Retention: active runs are never touched; finished runs are archived, then deleted** | Defaults, configurable per tenant: a finished run's journal stays 30 days in Postgres (or Turso), then is archived to S3 as a journal document for 1 year and loaded on demand; the read model keeps 90 days; `DELETE /v1/runs/{id}` erases a run at any time; payloads unreferenced for 24 h are deleted, using a `payload_refs` table filled at commit (closes question 11; [storage](06-storage-and-scale.md#retention-and-deletion)). |
| **Leases are renewed by a heartbeat during an activation** | The lease stays short (30 s) and the worker renews it about every 10 s while it lives, during the replay as during the execution. A long replay keeps its lease; a dead worker frees the run at most 30 s after its last renewal. The fence still refuses the commit of a worker that lost its lease. |
| **The latency of the polling path is accepted** | A script of 5 sequential calls makes about ten store round trips. Gains come from caching (instances, histories, payloads), not from a separate short path. |
| **The embedded CLI** | Schema versioned with migrations in the binary, refusing a newer schema; `--json` on every command and one exit code per outcome; a first Ctrl-C finishes the call in flight and commits it, a second one exits at once. |
| **No milestones: 0.1 is built as one product** | Version 0.1 is the whole specification. Everything is coded directly in its final shape, with no interim version of a feature. The plan is in [09](09-roadmap.md). |
| **Tools are called by Rewind, except host tools** | MCP tools and the built-ins `http` and `webhook` are dispatched by Rewind, and their results go back to the script, never to the agent. The harness is on the path only for its host tools, long-polled by the SDK worker. Host tools are optional: a toolset may have only MCP servers. No other kind of tool in 0.1 ([tools](04-tools.md#routes)). Extended by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026): API tools are a fourth route. |
| **`rewind-codegen` turns each pinned tool into three outputs** | The `rewind:tools` module, `rewind-tools.d.ts` and the compact prompt rendering. The JSON Schema → TypeScript converter is ours, in Rust: no maintained crate exists. Args are also validated at run time against the same schema, inside the op ([tools](04-tools.md#toolsets-and-code-generation)). |
| **The engine ports of 0.1** | `JournalStore`, `BlobStore` and `ToolDispatcher` extended; `TypeChecker`, `ReadModel`, `SpanExporter`, `SecretStore` and `WebhookSender` added; `ScriptExecutor`, `Clock` and `Entropy` unchanged, the instance cache living in the engine. Every port has a conformance suite in `rewind-engine` that all its adapters pass ([07](07-code-architecture.md#ports)). |
| **The stored format v1 covers four documents** | The event, the journal document, the cassette and the pinned toolset. Labels, idempotency keys, tenant, trace context, credentials, quarantine, lease, fence, sticky worker, feed and a fork's link to its parent stay out: table columns, never replayed, exported or put in a cassette ([02](02-journal.md#stored-format-v1)). |
| **HTTP API `/v1`** | An API key per tenant as Bearer; `Idempotency-Key` on run creation and signals; errors as `application/problem+json` with a message written for a model; the OpenAPI document generated from the DTOs (utoipa), and the SDK's types from the OpenAPI ([07](07-code-architecture.md#http-api-v1)). |
| **Embedded mode: the CLI is an API client of `rewind server --embedded`** | The CLI never opens `journal.db`. The embedded server is started on demand, holds `.rewind/server.lock`, listens on `.rewind/server.sock` (mode 0600, no key, one tenant `local`), and stops after 10 minutes idle. `rewind check`, `rewind test` and the replay of an exported journal run in the CLI process ([07](07-code-architecture.md#embedded-server-and-cli)). |
| **A thread per live instance** | With the bounded sticky cache. An isolate pool per thread only if the load tests fail ([execution](03-execution.md#sticky-execution)). |
| **Sticky execution** | After a commit the worker keeps the instance in an LRU cache (500 instances and 4 GiB per worker by default). The run's sticky worker gets its next activation first; other workers take it after 5 s. A hit delivers only the new trigger; a miss starts from a V8 startup snapshot and replays (closes question 2; [execution](03-execution.md#sticky-execution)). |
| **Payloads in three tiers** | Up to 1 KiB in the event; up to 64 KiB in the `payloads` table (Postgres, Turso); above, in S3 or files, with size and preview in `payloads`. Thresholds configurable, defaults confirmed by load tests ([storage](06-storage-and-scale.md#payloads)). |
| **Fork semantics** | A fork is a new run with its own id, the parent's seed, input (overridable) and toolset, and a new TTL. While its commands match, the parent's entries are copied and no tool is called again; at the first difference, at `at`, or at the end of the parent's history, it continues live with keys `{fork_run_id}/{seq}`. A call the parent left without an outcome is dispatched again with the parent's key under `retry`, and fails with `outcomeUnknown` under `at_most_once`. The link to the parent is run metadata (closes question 9; [execution](03-execution.md#forks)). |
| **One state machine for tool tasks** | `approval` → `ready` → `claimed` → `done`, with `deferred` (a token, for host tools that answer later) and `parked` (schema changed, 401). Per-tool policy: `retry` or `at_most_once`, `approval`, execution timeout (5 min for MCP), heartbeat timeout (60 s for host tools), concurrency (16 per run, 8 per MCP server on each worker). A terminal run's tasks are deleted ([tools](04-tools.md#tool-task-lifecycle)). |
| **Credentials are resolved by the dispatcher at call time** | Sent per toolset or per run, stored encrypted in the `SecretStore` (a data key per tenant, encrypted by a master key from the deployment's configuration), injected into the MCP transport. Never in the journal, the isolate, a cassette or OpenTelemetry. A 401 parks the task and sends `credentials.expired` ([tools](04-tools.md#authenticating-calls)). Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |
| **API keys and tenancy** | Superseded in part by [accounts and access](#taken-for-accounts-organizations-and-access-november-2026): rights come from grants. Keys `rewind_sk_…`, stored hashed, each with a tenant and a scope (a role, and optionally allowed labels). The tenant is part of every storage key. A quota of active runs per tenant (429 above it). Workers draw a tenant at random among those with runnable work, then take its oldest runnable run ([storage](06-storage-and-scale.md#tenancy-namespaces-identity-and-access)). |
| **Egress of the `http` tool** | Requests leave from the dispatcher. No allow-list, no `http` tool and no `fetch`. Each request: allow-list, one DNS resolution, private and metadata IPs refused, connection to the checked IP, each redirect checked again (5 at most), response bounded to 5 MB and 30 s; an egress proxy in production ([tools](04-tools.md#the-http-tool-and-egress)). |
| **Sandboxed workers** | One binary, two roles: `--role api` (no V8) and `--role worker`. Workers run under gVisor, in pools per tenant when needed, reach the outside only through the egress proxy, and have no route to the cloud metadata endpoint. Three walls between two tenants: V8, gVisor, separate pools ([storage](06-storage-and-scale.md#sandboxed-nodes)). |
| **The dashboard is a React SPA served by `rewind-server`** | It uses only the public API ([05](05-testing-and-observability.md#dashboard)). |
| **Evals: Claude Opus, Sonnet and Haiku, and one OpenAI model** | Run on demand, not in CI: they cost model tokens ([05](05-testing-and-observability.md#evals)). |
| **CLI exit codes** | 0 completed or success, 1 failed, 2 cancelled, 3 aborted, 4 still waiting, 5 quarantined, 6 refused, 64 usage, 65 script refused at start, 70 internal error, 130 second Ctrl-C ([07](07-code-architecture.md#embedded-server-and-cli)). |
| **Out of 0.1** | `rewind replay --inspect`; `continueAsNew` (a run that reaches the history cap of 50k entries is aborted with `limit_exceeded`; closes question 6); a columnar read model; sharding over several Postgres clusters. |
| **Type-checking uses tsgo (TypeScript 7), driven as a CLI** | TypeScript 7.0 has no programmatic API. The tsgo binary is embedded in the `rewind` binary and extracted to a cache directory at first launch, so Rewind stays a single binary. The checker's own rules run on the swc syntax tree that start already parses: syntactic, and slightly less precise than rules based on types ([tools](04-tools.md#type-checking)). |

### Taken for the dashboard's debugger and search (November 2026)

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **The debugger is a time cursor on the Run page** | At the last step it is the Run page as before; stepping back changes what the source, the notes and the timeline show. Not a separate page ([05](05-testing-and-observability.md#dashboard)). |
| **A hover shows only what crossed the journal** | Inputs, arguments, results, signal payloads, the output, and the variables bound directly to an awaited call. No replay debugger evaluating any expression: it would be an endpoint that executes code, which a `reader` key should not reach. |
| **A label is written `@key:value` in a query** | Short, and never confused with a field as fields are added ([05](05-testing-and-observability.md#query-language)). |
| **A query is terms joined by "and", "or" inside a term, "not" per term** | No parentheses and no "or" between terms: each term is a typed predicate that an index serves. |
| **Free text searches error messages, scripts' first lines, label values and run ids** | Logs and payloads are never indexed: payloads may hold sensitive data, and the spec already keeps their values out of OpenTelemetry. |
| **Saved searches are kept in the browser** | The dashboard keeps sending only `GET`, so a `reader` key is enough. Superseded in part by [accounts and access](#taken-for-accounts-organizations-and-access-november-2026): the dashboard also writes, for those who administer an organization. Superseded by [no saved searches](#taken-for-the-dashboards-sidebar-and-run-page-november-2026) (23 November 2026). |

### Taken for accounts, organizations and access (November 2026)

Decided by the owner on 14 November 2026, with the design reviewed the same day
([06](06-storage-and-scale.md#tenancy-namespaces-identity-and-access)).

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **WorkOS holds identity, and only identity** | Users, organizations, memberships, invitations, the waitlist, sessions. Never what someone may do: no role or permission is configured there, and Rewind reads neither a membership's role nor the access token's `role`, `roles` and `permissions` claims. |
| **Access is Rewind's: a catalog of permissions, roles and grants** | One mechanism for members and API keys. Four built-in roles (`owner`, `harness`, `worker`, `reader`); each organization composes its own from the catalog. |
| **A WorkOS organization is a tenant, with the same id** | Creating an account means joining an organization or creating one. |
| **Sign in with GitHub or a 6-digit code sent by email** | WorkOS Magic Auth. WorkOS's former Magic Link API is deprecated (mail scanners opened the links and used the code) and knows neither users nor organizations. |
| **On the dashboard's own pages** | Not the hosted AuthKit: same origin, same look, no redirect but GitHub's. |
| **Sign-ups go through the WorkOS waitlist** | The operator approves each person, and the approval sends an invitation. Approved people run code on the workers, behind the walls of the deployment they use. |
| **One joins an organization by email invitation** | Sent by a member who holds `members:invite`, with a role. |
| **WorkOS's variables keep WorkOS's names** | `WORKOS_API_KEY`, `WORKOS_CLIENT_ID`, `WORKOS_COOKIE_PASSWORD`, `WORKOS_REDIRECT_URI`, as in the owner's other projects. |

Approved with the design:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Two ports in `rewind-engine`, `IdentityProvider` and `AccessStore`; `rewind-workos` adapts the first, the stores the second | A trait in `rewind-server` | Every port lives in the engine (07); an adapter cannot depend on a composition root. The in-memory adapters drive the server's tests. |
| API keys move from `JournalStore` to `AccessStore`, beside roles and grants | Growing `JournalStore` | Who may do what is not the journal's business: the engine never read a key. |
| `/v1` stays Bearer only; the dashboard's session lives in a sealed cookie that only `/auth` reads | A session cookie on `/v1` | No forged request can reach `/v1`; the dashboard and harnesses authenticate the same way; the event stream already sends a header. |
| Every request reads the caller's grant | Permissions carried in the access token | A grant removed applies at the next request, and WorkOS knows nothing of rights. One store read per request, as the key lookup already was. |
| The key role `admin` becomes `operator`, outside every organization | Keeping `admin` | Organizations name their own roles; the operator's reach spans every tenant's keys. |
| No policy engine (`cedar-policy`, `casbin`) | A policy language | A check is a permission in a set plus labels that the list endpoints push into SQL; a policy engine evaluates one resource at a time. The check stays behind one call, `Caller::require`. |

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Payload values are part of `runs:read`, with no permission of their own | `payloads:read` | Entries, traces and the event stream carry values; a separate permission would have to strip them from each. |
| A grant limited to labels cannot administer (members, roles, keys, the organization) | Administering within its labels | Members, roles and keys carry no labels. |
| An invitation's grant has the principal `invitation:<id>`, claimed by the user WorkOS records as having accepted it (`accepted_user_id`) | A grant keyed by email | The accepting account's email may differ from the invited one (GitHub). |
| A member's first grant is written at their first session in the organization (`POST /auth/session`, accepting an invitation), never deduced at request time | The default role when no grant exists | Removing a grant must cut access, not fall back to the default role. |
| Creating an organization compensates: if the tenant or the owner's grant cannot be written, the organization is deleted in WorkOS | Leaving it behind | No organization without an owner. |
| Every `/auth` answer is an `AuthState`; the callback redirects to `/#/`, or to `/?auth_error=<code>#/` when the sign-in did not go through, and the dashboard asks `POST /auth/session` | Steps and tokens in the redirect's query | No token or pending step in a URL, a history or a log; the code names no secret. Since [the plain URLs](#taken-for-the-dashboards-plain-urls-november-2026), to `/` and `/?auth_error=<code>`. |
| The cookie is sealed with the `workos` crate's `seal` (AES-256-GCM), whose password is 64 hexadecimal characters | Sealing of our own | Take the maintained crate. |
| The `workos` SDK brings `reqwest` 0.12 beside the workspace's 0.13 | Calling WorkOS's HTTP API by hand | The official crate; the cost is build time. |
| WorkOS Staging, measured on 14 November 2026: the issuer has no trailing slash, the token carries `client_id`, access tokens live 300 s, refresh tokens do not rotate; an invitation creates the WorkOS user at once; WorkOS's demo organization holds `example.com` for SSO, so tests use `example.net` | — | What the code relies on, checked against the real service. |
| `AccessStore` answers the journal's `StoreError` | An error type of its own | The same backends fail the same ways: unreachable, or a conflict (a key's hash stored twice). |
| `keep` refuses only a write that takes the kept role away from the last member (`user:`) holding it; keys and pending invitations never count as holders | Refusing every write that leaves the tenant without an owner; counting every principal | Tenants whose keys the operator made have no owner, and their keys still get grants. An owner key or an invitation that expires cannot stand for the person who administers the organization. |
| `claim_grant`: a user who already has a grant keeps it, and the invitation's is deleted; without the invitation's grant it also answers `AlreadyGranted` | Replacing the user's grant | Accepting an invitation never changes what a member may do; `members:manage` does. |
| A replaced role keeps its first `created_at`. Lists are ordered: keys by creation then hash, roles by name, grants by principal as their text sorts | The server reading the role before writing it; unordered lists | One write, and the same answer from every store. |
| A principal's id (`user:`, `invitation:`) is 1 to 128 ASCII letters, digits, `-` and `_` | Any string | It is part of a key in every store, as a tenant id is; WorkOS's ids fit. |
| `IdentityProvider`'s gets answer `None` for an unknown id, its other methods `NotFound`; `authorization_url` is synchronous | `NotFound` everywhere; an asynchronous `authorization_url` | A missing invitation is an answer (404), not a failure; the URL is built without a call. |
| `MemoryIdentity` behaves as WorkOS: an access token stays valid until it expires after its session is revoked (a JWT checked locally), refresh tokens do not rotate, an invitation creates its user; time comes from an injected `Clock` | A fake that cuts tokens at sign-out | The server's tests must not rely on what WorkOS does not do. |
| `GET /v1/whoami` has no `tenant` for a member without organization and no `principal` over the socket; its `organization` carries the default role | A `GET /v1/organization` | The dashboard reads the default role where it reads the organization; 07 has no such endpoint. |
| `POST /auth/magic-auth` answers 202 without a body when it sent a code, else the step `sign_up_closed` (an `AuthState`, kept in the cookie); `POST /auth/waitlist` 202, `POST /auth/logout` 204 | Always 202, the same answer whether the email has an account or not | WorkOS refuses to send a code to an email without a user while its waitlist is on (measured on Production on 16 November 2026): a 202 would leave someone new waiting for a code that never comes. The owner chose to send them to the waitlist at once (16 November 2026), which tells that an email has no account, as GitHub's refusal does. |
| `GET /auth/github` takes `invitationToken`, in the API's camelCase; the callback keeps OAuth's `code`, `state`, `error` and `error_description` | `invitation_token` | The API's convention; OAuth's names are the provider's. |
| The OpenAPI document has two security schemes: `bearer` (a key or an access token) on `/v1`, `session` (the cookie) on the `/auth` steps that need it | One scheme | Readers and generated clients see which credential each route takes. |
| A key stored with the role `admin` is the operator's: the stores' migration 2 writes its grant as `operator`, and the server reads no `admin` role. The key DTOs carry their grant's role and labels inline and `createdBy` as a principal's text (`key:<id>`, `user:<id>`); `CreateApiKey` without `tenant` is for the caller's tenant | Migrating stored roles first; the user's details in `createdBy` | Keys made before roles became data keep working; listing keys needs no call to WorkOS. |
| `rewind-workos` reads a closed sign-up from one WorkOS code, `sign_up_not_allowed` (`SIGN_UP_CLOSED`, the only place codes of sign-up are listed); `send_magic_code` answers `MagicCode::SignUpClosed` when WorkOS refuses the Magic Auth for that reason, and a sign-in refused for it (GitHub's code) answers `SignUpClosed` | Guessing more codes; an error from `send_magic_code` | WorkOS documents no code for a sign-up refused through its API while its waitlist is on. Measured on Production on 16 November 2026: Create Magic Auth for an email without a user answers 400 `{"error": "sign_up_not_allowed", "error_description": "Creating new users is not allowed."}`, and creates nothing. How it refuses an unknown GitHub account (at the callback, or when its code is exchanged) is not measured: either way the dashboard leads to the waitlist. |
| WorkOS's 401 (the API key refused), 429, 5xx and network failures are `Unavailable`; other 4xx are `Refused` with WorkOS's code (`error` for its OAuth errors). A 404 is `NotFound`, named by WorkOS's `entity_id`, but in a sign-in it is `Refused`: what is missing there is a token or an organization. The SDK's retries stay: reads retried on 429 and 5xx, writes sent once | Every 4xx `Refused`; no retry | Neither a refused key nor a rate limit is the caller's mistake. A sign-in names no object, as `MemoryIdentity` refuses an unknown invitation. |
| On a refresh, `invalid_grant` and WorkOS's session codes are `InvalidToken`; on a step, `invalid_pending_authentication_token` is. On GitHub's code, `invalid_grant` is `Refused` | One mapping for every grant | WorkOS answers `invalid_grant` both for a refresh token whose session ended and for a spent OAuth code (measured on Staging); `InvalidToken` is about the port's own tokens. |
| A message of WorkOS is passed on with every secret the call sent (code, pending token, refresh token, invitation token) replaced by `<redacted>`; a transport error leaves the URL out | Passing messages on as they are; dropping them | WorkOS repeats the pending token, the OAuth code and the invitation token in some messages (measured on Staging). |
| An access token also needs a `sid`, and is refused with a `sub_type`, or a `sub_profile` other than `user`. A key of the JWKS is used only if it is the one RSA key of its `kid` (`alg` RS256 and `use` `sig` when given) | Checking only the issuer, the client and the expiry | WorkOS signs other kinds of tokens (agents, widgets) with the same key, which the `workos` SDK's own session check refuses too. |
| The JWKS is the SDK's helper, cached 10 minutes; a token with an unknown `kid` fetches it again at most every 10 s; the last set fetched serves while WorkOS cannot be reached | A fetch for every unknown `kid`; failing once the cache is older than 10 minutes and WorkOS is down | Forged tokens cannot make each request call WorkOS, a rotated key is still found within 10 s, and an outage of WorkOS does not sign members out of `/v1`. |
| `members` and `memberships_of_user` list the active memberships only; every list is read in creation order, by pages of 100 | Every status | WorkOS creates a pending membership with each invitation to an organization (measured on Staging): the invitation stands for it, as in `MemoryIdentity`. |
| `join_waitlist` adds to WorkOS's waitlist `default` and asks for its confirmation email, which WorkOS sends only when the environment enables it. An email that already has a user, which WorkOS refuses (`user_already_exists`, 422), gets the same answer as a new one | Looking the waitlist's id up; never asking; passing the refusal on | `default` names the environment's waitlist, which WorkOS creates at its first entry (measured on Staging, then on Production on 16 November 2026); whether people get a confirmation is the environment's setting. The answer says nothing of an account, as for a code; an approved person already has a user, created by the approval's invitation (measured on Production), and the page that answers tells them to sign in. |
| WorkOS Staging, measured on 14 November 2026 by `rewind-workos`: Create Magic Auth creates the user and answers the code; an organization's deletion answers 202 and takes its invitations; an invitation without organization cannot be deleted, only revoked; resending a pending invitation keeps its expiry, which `MemoryIdentity` renews | — | What the adapter and its Staging test rely on, and where the port's fake differs. |
| Keys leave the journal by a migration of each store (Postgres migration 2, Turso schema version 2): each key of version 1 gets a grant `key:<hex>` of its role and allowed labels, granted at the key's creation and by no one, `admin` becoming `operator`; it is named `unnamed <role> key` and has no recorded creator. The server reads no `admin` any more | The server reading `admin` as an alias of `operator`; an empty name | An organization may name a role `admin`: an alias would make it the operator's. A name says what a key is, in the list. |
| A role's permissions are a `text[]` in Postgres and a sorted JSON array in Turso; principals are stored as their text, whose byte order is theirs | A table of a role's permissions | The store never reads them (07); one column, read and written whole. |
| On Postgres, every write of a tenant's grants (`put_grant`, `delete_grant`, `claim_grant`) and `delete_role` take a transaction-scoped advisory lock on the tenant before they read and write | Locking the tenant's `access_settings` row; `SERIALIZABLE` | A tenant without settings has no row to lock. Two owners demoting each other at once are decided in turn, without retries; grant writes are rare. |
| Revoking a key deletes its grant. A key without a grant is listed with the role `""` and no labels | Keeping a revoked key's grant | A revoked key's grant would hold its role in use for ever (`delete_role` answers `InUse`) and gives nothing. |
| Creating a key writes the key, then its grant; if the grant cannot be written, the key is revoked and the request fails | One transaction across both | `AccessStore` has no transaction across methods; a key without a grant holds nothing, and once revoked it never authenticates. |
| A key is revoked by a caller of its tenant, or by the operator for any tenant; another tenant's key is 404. Revoking takes the key's grant away, so it needs every permission of the key's role, and only the operator revokes an operator key. `GET /v1/api-keys` lists the caller's tenant only, the operator's too | Listing every tenant's keys to the operator | The operator reaches the keys of every tenant by creating and revoking them (06); the list has no tenant to name. |
| The key of `--admin-key` is named `operator key (--admin-key)`; at each start its grant is written again as `operator` without labels when it is missing or was changed | Writing the grant only with the key | The key given at start is the operator's by configuration (06). |
| A key without a grant authenticates and holds no permission: 403, naming the permission the request needs, and `GET /v1/whoami` answers it without `grant`. A role's stored permission the catalog does not know gives nothing | 401 | The key is genuine; what it lacks is a right, as for a member without a grant. |
| A key's name is trimmed and may not be blank | Any string | A key's name says what it is for: blank, the list says nothing. |
| `rewind-workos` exposes the sealing as `CookieSeal` (the `workos` crate's `seal` and `unseal`, its password checked at start); `rewind-server` wires `WorkOs` or, in its tests, `MemoryIdentity` (`start_with_identity`) behind one enum, `wiring::identity` | The server depending on `workos` itself | The server depends on one adapter of WorkOS, and the seal's password is checked where WorkOS's other settings are. |
| The four `WORKOS_*` are checked together (all or none, a 64-hex cookie password, an `http(s)://` redirect URI: exit 64) on every deployed server, and used by the api role only: a worker alone logs that they are not used, and the embedded server ignores them | Refusing them on a worker; checking them on the embedded server | A worker accepts only keys. A developer's shell may hold WorkOS's variables, which keep WorkOS's names, for another project: the CLI's embedded server must start anyway. |
| A failed callback redirects to `/?auth_error=<code>#/`: `state_mismatch` (no GitHub sign-in in progress in this browser, its cookie expired, or another `state`), `provider_error` (an `error` from GitHub or WorkOS, or no code), `sign_in_failed` (the code refused, or WorkOS unavailable). It leaves `rewind_session` as it was; `rewind_oauth` is cleared either way, and holds its own expiry, sealed | Recording the failure in the session cookie for `POST /auth/session` | The dashboard says why without a call; a forged callback can sign no one in nor out, and a replayed `rewind_oauth` dies after 5 minutes whatever the browser keeps. Since [the plain URLs](#taken-for-the-dashboards-plain-urls-november-2026), `/?auth_error=<code>`. |
| `rewind_session` holds, sealed, the tokens with the session's id, the user, the organization and the token's expiry, or the pending step with what its `AuthState` shows (the user and organizations, the email). It lasts 30 days (`Max-Age`); every `/auth` answer is `Cache-Control: no-store` | Reading the user and the expiry from WorkOS at each `POST /auth/session` | `POST /auth/session` calls WorkOS only when a refresh is due; 30 days is WorkOS's longest session, which ends sooner when WorkOS ends it. |
| `POST /auth/session` refreshes when less than 60 s are left by the server's real clock (also on a server in virtual time). A refresh refused or with an invalid token ends the session: 401, the cookie cleared. A switch first checks an active membership (`memberships_of_user`, 403 otherwise), and `POST /auth/organization` checks the organization against those the step offered (403) | Mapping WorkOS's error codes to 403 | WorkOS documents no code for "not a member"; the check is one call, and the switch is rare. |
| The first grant is written at every answer `authenticated` on an organization (the steps, the callback, `POST /auth/session`) when the member has none: only for an active member of it (`memberships_of_user`), then the pending grant of their latest accepted invitation of it that has one (`claim_grant`), else the default role. A step or the callback that cannot write it still signs in, and the next `POST /auth/session` writes it (its error is answered) | Writing it only after a refresh; trusting the access token's organization | A token issued before a removal is still valid for minutes: the membership check keeps a removed member from getting the default role back. One store read when a grant exists. |
| Accepting an invitation (`POST /v1/invitations/{id}/accept`) takes a pending invitation to an organization whose email is the caller's (case-insensitive), else 404; then `claim_grant`, and without the invitation's grant the first grant. The answer's `grant` is the caller's grant in the organization. `GET /v1/invitations` lists the pending invitations to an organization only | Answering the pending grant, gone once claimed | An invitation to the deployment (the waitlist's) is accepted by signing up with its link. The dashboard shows what the member now holds. |
| A member without organization gets 403 on every `/v1` endpoint but the four of onboarding, telling to create or choose one; keys and the socket's operator get 403 on the three onboarding endpoints that need a person (creating an organization, the invitations sent to me, accepting one) | 401; letting a key create an organization | The token is genuine; the organization is what is missing. A key has no email, no membership, no session. |
| `GET /v1/whoami`'s `permissions` are the effective ones: a grant limited to labels lists its role's without the six of the administration | The role's permissions | The dashboard shows what the caller may use (06's labels rule). |
| Without WorkOS, the organization, member and invitation endpoints answer 404 (`not-found`, saying the server has no sign-in), and `/auth/*` too; roles, keys and grants work | 403 | They name WorkOS's objects, which such a server has none of. |
| `PATCH /v1/organization`'s `defaultRole` is a built-in role or one of the organization's, never `operator` (400 otherwise), and the caller holds every permission of it (403). The default role cannot be deleted (409 `role-in-use`) | Letting a deleted role be the default | A member's first grant would give nothing; making a role the default gives it to everyone who joins. |
| Changing a role (`PUT /v1/roles/{name}` of an existing one) needs every permission it gives and every permission it gave; `PUT` answers 201 when it creates, 200 when it changes; built-in names and `operator` are 400 on `PUT` and `DELETE` | Only the permissions given | A change takes permissions away from every holder at once: no one takes away more than they hold. |
| Inviting writes the WorkOS invitation, with the caller as inviter when a member, then the pending grant; if the grant cannot be written, the invitation is revoked. Revoking an invitation needs every permission of its pending grant's role, and only a pending one is revoked (404 otherwise); an invitation of another organization is 404 | One transaction across both; revoking any state | An invitation without its grant would let its invitee in with the default role. |
| Removing a member deletes the grant (`keep = owner`), then the membership; a failure of WorkOS after the grant is deleted answers 502, and the request is repeated | The membership first | The grant cuts access at the next request; a member left without a grant holds nothing, and `DELETE` again finishes. |
| The address passed to WorkOS is the last of `X-Forwarded-For` (the edge's Caddy writes it and ignores one a client sends, `deploy/`), else the connection's peer, and only when it is a public address: a private, shared (`100.64.0.0/10`), loopback, link-local or unique-local one is a proxy of the deployment. The user agent is cut at 512 characters | Every address; the first of `X-Forwarded-For` | A client can write the first; behind Railway's proxy and Vercel's rewrite the edge sees a proxy, and one shared address would make WorkOS's Radar treat every member as one. |
| An event stream opened with an access token ends at the token's expiry by real time, without an `end` event | Checking the grant again while streaming | 06: the dashboard resumes it with a new token and `from`, which reads the grant again. |
| `POST /auth/logout` clears the cookie even when WorkOS cannot revoke the session, and then answers 502; it takes `{}` or an empty body | 204 whatever happened | The browser is signed out; the dashboard can say the session expires at WorkOS on its own. |
| `GET /auth/config` answers `waitlist: true` | Asking WorkOS | Sign-ups are closed on every deployment with WorkOS (06); WorkOS has no API to read the setting. |
| A member's tenant record is created at their request when missing, and the server remembers in memory the tenants it has seen | A store read per request | A tenant is never deleted; the grant is still read at every request. |
| `rewind-workos` gives an invitation's token to WorkOS with the code only (`authenticate_magic_code`), never when the code is sent: `send_magic_code` ignores it, as `MemoryIdentity` does, and an unknown or spent token is refused with the code (`invitation_invalid`) | Passing the token to Create Magic Auth too | WorkOS accepts the invitation, and activates its membership, as soon as a Magic Auth is created with its token, before anyone has proved the email is theirs; the authentication that then carries the token is refused, `invitation_invalid` (measured on Staging on 14 November 2026, found by criterion 8's acceptance). An invited person's user exists from the invitation, so the code is sent to them without the token (measured on Production with the Waitlist on, 16 November 2026) |
| `rewind-workos` looks an invitation's token up (`find_invitation_by_token`) before a sign-in that carries it, and does not send the token of an invitation without organization; an unknown token is sent, for WorkOS to refuse | Sending every token; failing the sign-in | WorkOS refuses a sign-in that carries the token of an invitation to the deployment (`invitation_invalid`, though the invitation is pending) and signs its invitee in without it, which accepts the invitation (measured on Staging and Production on 16 November 2026). Such an invitation gives no membership and no grant: the token adds nothing. |
| An approved person signs in on the dashboard with the approved email, by a code or with GitHub; the approval's invitation email is WorkOS's | Inviting through Rewind after approving | WorkOS's approval links its invitation to hosted AuthKit (`<domain>.authkit.app/invite`), whatever the *User invitation URL*, which applies to the invitations Rewind sends (measured on Production on 16 November 2026). The approval creates the user, so signing in on the dashboard needs no token. A sign-in completed on AuthKit comes back to `/auth/callback` without the dashboard's `state`, which refuses it (`state_mismatch`): the page then asks to sign in again. |
| Criterion 8's acceptance reads no mailbox: the code it types is one WorkOS's Create Magic Auth answers for the email, after the dashboard had WorkOS send its own, and the invitation's link is the dashboard with the token read from WorkOS's API | A mailbox service, or a catch-all domain | Any valid code of the email signs in, whoever asked for it; `example.net` receives no mail, and the test already holds WorkOS's API |
| The acceptance's server has its own origin's `/auth/callback` as `WORKOS_REDIRECT_URI`, registered nowhere, and a `WORKOS_COOKIE_PASSWORD` made for each run; Chromium opens its dashboard on `localhost` | Registering the URI through WorkOS's API; serving the dashboard on `localhost:5173` | Only a sign-in with GitHub goes to the redirect URI, and no test drives GitHub: the server checks the URI's shape only. The sign-in's cookies are `Secure`, which browsers keep over `http://` on `localhost` only |
| `acceptance/run.sh` reads Staging's `WORKOS_API_KEY` and `WORKOS_CLIENT_ID` from the environment and they reach criterion 8's server only: its own steps run without any `WORKOS_*`, and the specs' processes inherit none | Every process inheriting them | A server given two of the four exits with 64, and the server's Rust tests read the four from the environment as its options do |
| Without them, criterion 8 is NOT RUN in `summary.md`, with the reason, and the run does not pass; a spec skipped for want of them is reported the same way | Skipped as a pass | A criterion is proven or not, and the summary says which and why |
| Criterion 8's clean-up removes the users whose ids Create Magic Auth answered and those WorkOS lists for the run's emails, the organizations of their memberships and the one read from the dashboard (only if the run named it), and the pending invitations of the emails; then it checks that nothing is left, waiting up to 30 s for WorkOS | Removing only what WorkOS lists for the emails | Found by its email only, a run's owner was once left behind on Staging (WorkOS's lists may lag a write), and a failure before an id is read leaves nothing behind either. No other organization, WorkOS's demo one among them, is touched |
| "Each endpoint refuses a caller without its permission" is proven at acceptance by one request per permission of the catalog (checked against `GET /v1/permissions`), for a `harness` key and a `reader` member: 403 naming the permission outside their role, an answer within it. Every endpoint for every permission is `rewind-server`'s `tests/access.rs` | Every endpoint for every caller in the acceptance | The acceptance proves real callers end to end, through WorkOS; the full matrix runs in the server's tests without it |

### Taken for namespaces (November 2026)

Decided by the owner on 14 November 2026, with the design reviewed the same day
([06](06-storage-and-scale.md#namespaces)).

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **A namespace isolates one end user of a customer** | Not an environment. A customer (a tenant) serves its own users; the agent acting for Alice cannot see or reach Bob's runs, whatever its model writes. Namespaces also protect the platform against an end user who abuses the customer's product. |
| **The customer holds all rights on its namespaces** | Its members and keys keep their grants ([accounts and access](#taken-for-accounts-organizations-and-access-november-2026)); there is no grant per namespace. |
| **End users get tokens, in two forms** | Opaque tokens minted through the API, and JWTs the customer signs with a key it registered. |
| **A workflow is a distinct script** | The quota on workflows counts the distinct scripts (by digest) with a run kept in the namespace: a stock, not the new scripts of a month. There is no named workflow. |
| **Six quotas** | CPU per month, active runs, runs per month and the longest TTL; distinct scripts; the longest script, in lines. |
| **CPU time above its quota alerts the customer, and nothing more** | Nothing is refused or paused. The other quotas refuse. |
| **Credentials per end user** | An end user's credentials for a toolset are resolved after the run's and before the toolset's. Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |
| **`Rewind-Namespace` is required to create** | There is no default namespace: every run belongs to an end user. |

Approved with the design:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The customer names the namespace with a header, `Rewind-Namespace` | The namespace in every path (`/v1/namespaces/{name}/runs/…`) | Paths stay, and a read exists once for one namespace or for all; run ids are already unique per tenant. Stripe (`Stripe-Account`) and OpenAI (`OpenAI-Project`) do the same. |
| A token's namespace comes from the token, never from a parameter | A header the agent sets | Neither the agent nor the scripts its model writes can leave the end user. |
| Three levels of limits: the tenant's defaults and a namespace's exceptions, set by the customer; the tenant's ceiling, set by the operator | One level | Paying and blocked end users; the operator's offer. |
| `activeRuns`, `runsPerMonth` and `scripts` refuse a start (429); `maxTtl` (400) and `maxScriptLines` (422) refuse the request | Alerts for every quota | The owner asked that CPU only alert; the others are checked where the run is created. |
| Alerts at 80 % and 100 %, through the outbound webhooks (`quota.alert`) and in the dashboard | A channel of their own | The customer already receives signed webhooks. |
| CPU time counts the calls that execute, not those of a replay, and each worker writes it about every 10 s | An activation's whole CPU; a write per activation | A replay is the cost of the instance cache; a write per activation would contend on one row. A worker that dies loses at most 10 s. |
| **CPU time is not in the journal** | An entry per activation | The journal holds what a run did, the same at every replay; CPU time differs at each replay. It is metering, like service telemetry, kept in `usage`. |
| Toolsets and subscriptions stay the tenant's; tasks and events name their namespace | Per namespace | The customer registers its tools once and receives every webhook; its handler acts for `ctx.namespace`. |
| No namespace stage in the workers' fairness | Drawing a namespace after the tenant | With thousands of namespaces the draw costs; `activeRuns` bounds an end user's share of the queue. |
| `rewind-core` and `rewind-format` know nothing of namespaces | A field of `run_started` | It is run metadata, like the tenant and labels, and never changes what a run does. |

Taken while freezing the contracts:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A namespace's name is 1 to 128 letters, digits, `-`, `_`, `.`, `:` and `@`, case-sensitive; `*` stands for the tenant in `usage` and `quota_alerts`. A namespace is created at first use | DNS labels; creating them explicitly | It is the customer's id for the end user, often a UUID, `user_…` or an email; a token or a run is enough to create one. |
| Opaque tokens are `rewind_nt_` and 43 base-62 characters, their SHA-256 is their id; 1 hour by default, 30 days at most | JWTs signed by Rewind | They reuse the keys' mechanism and are revoked one by one. |
| A signed token's header names a registered key, `kid` `rk_…`, a JWK (Ed25519 with EdDSA, P-256 with ES256, RSA with RS256); claims `sub` (the namespace), `aud` `rewind`, `iat` and `exp` at most 30 days apart, and optionally `permissions`, `labels`, `jti`. A key records the permissions its tokens may carry, among those its registrant holds | A JWKS URL fetched from the customer | No outbound request at authentication; a key is revoked by deleting it. |
| Revoking a namespace's tokens sets its `tokens_revoked_before`, which also refuses the signed tokens issued earlier | A list of revoked `jti` | One timestamp per namespace, read with the namespace. |
| A token holds a subset of `runs:read`, `runs:start`, `runs:control`, `runs:quarantine`, `runs:delete`, `toolsets:read`, `credentials:write`, `tasks:work` and `tasks:approve`; by default `runs:read`, `runs:start`, `runs:control` and `toolsets:read` | Any permission | Never administration, toolsets, subscriptions, namespaces or tokens. Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |
| `namespaces:read` goes to `owner`, `harness` and `reader`; `namespaces:write` and `tokens:write` to `owner` and `harness` | `owner` only | The customer's backend manages its end users without being an owner. |
| A key's or a member's read without the header covers every namespace; with one, that namespace, and a run of another is 404 | The header on every request | The customer holds every namespace, and the dashboard reads them all. |
| Existing rows move to a namespace named `migrated` | A default namespace | There is none, and the migration needs one name. |
| The CLI uses the namespace `local` against its own embedded server when none is given; against a remote server, a start without one is a usage error (64) | `local` everywhere | Local development needs no flag; a remote tenant's runs always name their end user. |
| The SDK binds a namespace with `rewind.namespace(name)`, takes a token with `new Rewind({ token })`, and gives handlers `ctx.namespace` | A `namespace` field in every call | One client per end user, as the header is per request. |
| A namespace being erased refuses runs and tokens: 409 `namespace-deleting` | Recreating it | A late request must not undo an erasure. |
| Erasing a namespace keeps the tenant's `*` usage | Subtracting it | Erasing end users must not reset the tenant's ceiling. |
| The tenant's `scripts` is the sum over its namespaces: a script run in two namespaces counts twice | Distinct scripts across the tenant | One counter per namespace is all the lock of `create_run` needs. |
| `--run-quota` becomes `--quota-active-runs`, `--quota-runs-per-month`, `--quota-cpu-per-month`, `--quota-scripts`, `--quota-max-ttl` and `--quota-max-script-lines` for the tenants a server creates; the operator changes a tenant's ceiling with `PUT /v1/organizations/{tenant}/quotas` | Changing store rows (replaces the decision of `--run-quota`) | A ceiling is the customer's offer, and offers change. |
| `GET /v1/organization/quota-alerts` lists the alerts of a month | Alerts only on a namespace's page | The Namespaces view shows the tenant's alerts. |
| Quota alerts are leased and acknowledged as feed items are (`claim_quota_alerts`, `ack_quota_alerts`); the event's id is `quota.alert:{namespace or *}:{quota}:{period}:{threshold}` | Sending them from the write that records them | Delivery stays in the worker role, at least once, and receivers deduplicate by id. |
| An exception is a limit or nothing: a quota absent from a namespace's exceptions follows the default, and an exception cannot lift a default to "no limit" | A third state for "unlimited" | One shape (`Quotas`) for defaults, exceptions and ceilings; a large limit serves a paying end user. |
| `create_run` checks `active_runs`, then `runs_per_month`, then `scripts`, each at the namespace then at the tenant, and answers the first reached. A run counts in the month of its `created_at`. The namespace is created even when the run is then refused | Checking the tenant first; creating the namespace only with a run | The namespace's limit is the one an end user can act on; a refused start is a first use. |
| A crossing is `before × 100 < limit × percent ≤ after × 100`, in integers (80 % of 4 runs is reached at the 4th, of 1 at the 1st; dividing first rounded the threshold down, to the 3rd, and never to 0); `record_usage` adds a namespace's CPU time only while the namespace exists, and always the tenant's | Floating point; refusing the CPU time of an erased namespace | Exact thresholds; the tenant's ceiling counts what was spent. |
| A quota alert carries no labels: a subscription limited to labels does not receive it. `WebhookEvent.run` is absent for a quota alert | Copying labels from the end user's runs | An alert is about a namespace, not a run. |
| The engine refuses a script above `max_script_lines` with `StartError::ScriptTooLong`, answered as `invalid-script` with the diagnostic `rewind/max-script-lines`; lines are the source's lines (`str::lines`) | Counting statements | What the model wrote, as it sees it. |
| A namespace's credentials are stored under the owner `namespace:<name>/toolset:<digest>`; a name holds no `/`, so erasing a namespace deletes the owners with its prefix | A table of their own | The stores keep one `secrets` table and its encryption. Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |
| The CLI takes `--namespace` (and `REWIND_NAMESPACE`) as a global option | A flag of each command | Every command that touches runs needs it. |

Taken while building the server and the CLI:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The server's tests run on the engine's in-memory adapters of the four store ports through `rewind_server::start_in_memory`, a switch `ServerArgs` does not read from the command line | A `--store memory:` option | No deployment keeps its store in memory; the tests drive the reference implementations of the contracts, as `start_with_identity` drives the fake WorkOS. |
| Both roles accept end users' tokens, with or without WorkOS | Workers accepting only API keys | `POST /v1/test` runs on the workers, and a token holding `runs:start` calls it; a token is checked against the access store alone. |
| A signed token is refused when its `iat`, in milliseconds, is before its namespace's `tokens_revoked_before`; its `exp` has 30 s of leeway, as a member's access token; `exp` comes after `iat`, by 30 days at most; `sub` must be a namespace's name | A list of revoked `jti`; no leeway | One comparison with the namespace read anyway; a token signed in the second of a revocation is refused, the customer signs a new one. |
| A token minted without `permissions`, or signed without the claim, holds the default permissions its author holds, or its key may give | Refusing it | A key registered for read-only tokens signs them without the claim; asking explicitly for more stays refused (403 at minting, 401 for a signed token). |
| A token key's `kid` is `rk_` and 24 base-62 characters (143 bits); Rewind keeps and answers the JWK's public parameters only (`kty`, `crv`, `x`, `y`, `n`, `e`); a JWK with `d`, `p`, `q`, `dp`, `dq`, `qi` or `oth`, an RSA modulus under 2048 bits, or another curve is refused (400) | Keeping the JWK as given | A `kid`, `alg` or `use` of the customer's JWK would contradict Rewind's; a private part sent by mistake is not stored. |
| A token of a namespace being erased is refused with 401, opaque or signed, and an opaque token's request reads its namespace | 409 `namespace-deleting`; relying on the revocation at erasure | Authentication refuses what no longer authenticates; a token minted while the erasure starts would otherwise outlive it. `namespace-deleting` answers what creates in the namespace: a run, a token, exceptions, credentials. |
| A request bound to a namespace (a token, or `Rewind-Namespace`) is narrowed everywhere: lists, statistics, failures, scripts, tool statistics, alerts, the list of namespaces; a run, a task, a token or a namespace of another one is missing (404), a task's token gone (410); a payload names its run (403 without) | Narrowing runs only | One rule for every read; the dashboard's namespace filter sends the header on every request. |
| `GET /v1/scripts/{digest}` checks `namespace_has_script` for a token, and the read model's runs of the namespace for a header | The read model for both | A token's boundary is the store's authoritative count; a header only narrows the customer's own reads, as labels do. |
| An end user's token never sets a toolset's credentials, and a token or a grant limited to labels never sets a namespace's (403) | Allowing them within the namespace | A toolset's credentials serve every end user, a namespace's every run of the end user. Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |
| The embedded server's socket mints no token and registers no key (403) | A principal for the socket | Tokens authenticate over HTTP, which the embedded server does not serve, and a token records the key or member who minted it. |
| `DELETE /v1/namespaces/{name}` answers 202 again for a namespace being erased, and does again what the first did: revoke its tokens, cancel its active runs (reason "the end user's namespace is being erased") | 409 | A retry finishes what a failure left. `DELETE /v1/tokens/{id}` of a revoked token answers 204 too. |
| `/v1/check` and `/v1/test` apply `maxScriptLines` (422) and lower a scenario's default TTL to `maxTtl`, without creating the namespace or refusing one being erased | Creating it at a check | A check creates nothing; the namespace's limits are read as a start reads them. |
| `GET /v1/namespaces`: `above` is a share from 0 to 100 rounded to a whole percent; the cursor is `{key}-{name}`, the last namespace's use in the order and its name | An opaque cursor | The key is digits only: the first `-` ends it, whatever the name holds. |
| `Retry-After` is the seconds left before `resetsAt` by the server's clock, at least 1, set by the router from the problem | An HTTP date | The SDK reads seconds; virtual time stays consistent with `resetsAt`. |
| `PUT /v1/organization/namespace-defaults` creates the tenant's record when missing, with the deployment's ceiling; `PUT /v1/organizations/{tenant}/quotas` answers 404 for a tenant without one | Creating it at the operator's request | A customer's first write is a first use of its tenant; the operator names tenants that exist. |
| The engine gains `set_namespace_credentials`, which stores an end user's credentials for a toolset and makes the calls parked by `credentials.expired` in the namespace's runs ready again (`ParkedScope::Namespace`) | The server writing the secret store | Credentials and parked calls are the engine's, as for a toolset or a run. Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |
| The CLI's `rewind namespaces` span namespaces: they send `Rewind-Namespace` only with `--namespace`, even against the embedded server. `quotas` changes the limits given and keeps the others, `none` removing one. `rewind run` on a remote server with an API key needs `--namespace` (64); with an end user's token it does not | `local` by default; `quotas` replacing every exception | An administrator reads every end user; `PUT` replaces, and a flag that resets the others would surprise. A token names its own namespace. |
| `--quota-cpu-per-month` and `--quota-max-ttl` take durations (`100h`), the four others counts; each also reads `REWIND_QUOTA_…` | Milliseconds | As `--default-ttl` and `--max-ttl`. |

Taken while building the engine, the runtime and the stores:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `ScriptInstance::cpu_time()` reports the CPU time of the instance's last call into the script, failed or not (loading the script and `main(input)` after `start`, one delivery after `deliver`), zero for an executor that does not measure it; the V8 executor takes it from the watchdog's clock (`CLOCK_THREAD_CPUTIME_ID`, armed to disarmed) | CPU time in what `start` and `deliver` return | The one port change, shaped as `memory_usage`; a call stopped by its CPU budget still reports what it used, which a value returned with the emitted events could not. |
| The engine, not the runtime, tells replay from execution: the calls of a replay are never counted; the calls that deliver an activation's new triggers are, and so are a fork's first calls, which follow its parent's history | The runtime flagging its calls; a fork's calls counted as a replay | The runtime only executes, replay lives in the engine; a fork's script runs for the first time, which is no cost of the instance cache. A fork's compatibility check runs no run and counts nothing. |
| A start that fails counts no CPU time | Carrying CPU time in `ExecutorError` | No instance remains to report it; the run ends at once, and that one call is bounded by the CPU budget (5 s). |
| The worker writes whole milliseconds and keeps the rest counted; a write that fails puts its deltas back | Rounding each call | `usage` counts milliseconds, and most calls take less than one. |
| The worker writes its CPU time from a loop of its own, every lease renewal period (10 s), and once when it stops | Writing from an activation's lease heartbeat | An idle worker still writes what its last activations counted; one write per worker and period whatever the activations. |
| The worker role sends quota alerts from a loop of its own: a batch claimed for its deliveries' window and a margin, sent at once, each delivery awaited; an alert is acknowledged once its deliveries ended, sent or given up | Queuing them with the engine's background sender | At least once, as lifecycle events: an alert whose deliveries a stop cut is sent again with the same id. |
| Erasing a namespace sends each active run one cancellation (idempotency key `rewind:namespace-erasure`, message "its namespace is being erased"), and erases its ended runs; a quarantined run is erased without waiting; once no run is left, the namespace's credentials go, then `drop_namespace`. The retention round (every 5 minutes) does it, through all of a namespace's runs | Waiting for quarantined runs; erasing active runs at once | The end user's runs end through the normal path, the customer receives their `run.cancelled`; a quarantined run takes no cancellation while it stays quarantined, and an erasure must not wait for an operator. |
| The stores keep the tenant's ceiling and its namespaces' defaults, and a namespace's exceptions, as a column per quota (the ceiling's active runs stays `active_run_quota`); a day as days since the Unix epoch, a period as `2026-11`, a threshold as its percent | A JSON document per set of limits | Postgres filters and orders the namespaces by their share of a limit in SQL, and pages them there. |
| Postgres takes a transaction-scoped advisory lock on the tenant in `create_run` and `record_usage` (in tenant order when it writes for several), then locks the namespace's row | Locking the `tenants` row; `SERIALIZABLE` | A tenant without a record has no row to lock; one lock per tenant serializes its starts and the writes of its use, and the fixed order leaves no cycle. Turso's one connection and its transaction are the lock. |
| The embedded store lists namespaces in memory; Postgres in SQL | The same SQL in both | The embedded server serves one developer and few namespaces. |
| The migration puts each tenant's runs in `migrated`, created with its script counts and the runs started per day, its own and its tenant's, from the runs' `created_at`; CPU time before it is unknown, and counts 0. Turso rebuilds `runs` for its new unique key, Postgres replaces the constraint | Starting `migrated` from zero | Its quotas start from what it really holds. SQLite cannot drop a table's constraint. |
| The conformance suite of `AccessStore` checks tokens and token keys: a hash or a `kid` stored once, found revoked or expired, revoked per tenant, per namespace, counted live before their expiry, keys listed by creation then `kid` and deleted per tenant | Leaving them to the server's tests | Every adapter of the port keeps them; the in-memory adapter is the reference. |

### Taken for Claude as a harness (November 2026)

Decided by the owner on 19 November 2026, with the design reviewed the same day
([01](01-product.md#claude-as-the-harness), [06](06-storage-and-scale.md#connections),
[07](07-code-architecture.md#mcp-and-oauth)).

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **Rewind is an MCP server that Claude adds as a connector** | `https://api.rewind.codes/mcp`, for claude.ai, Desktop, mobile and Claude Code: Claude is the harness. The dashboard guides the installation, up to the first call. |
| **Rewind issues its own tokens** | Its own OAuth authorization server, not WorkOS AuthKit's: rights stay in Rewind, and a token is a connection, which a WorkOS token naming only the user could not tell apart from the same person's other connections. WorkOS signs the person in on the consent page, nothing more. |
| **One key per person, for the organizations they tick** | A connection is a person's, not an organization's: it opens the organizations they choose, each with its access, never beyond their own rights there. |
| **Nothing else is pinned** | The organization, the namespace and the toolset are arguments of the tools, which Claude finds with `list_orgs`, `list_namespaces` and `list_toolsets`. |
| **No `save_workflow` over MCP** | Nor `toolsets:write` for a connection. |
| **What Claude starts carries `via: claude`** | Unticked at consent to debug other runs. |
| **No stdio bridge to a deployed server** | claude.ai and Desktop reach only remote servers, and Claude Code signs in with OAuth on the same URL; a bridge would keep a long-lived key in a configuration file, without consent nor revocation per person. |
| **`/mcp` also takes an API key or an end user's token** | For what has no browser: CI, headless agents, the Claude API's MCP connector. |
| **`rewind mcp`, over stdio, for debugging only** | Against the embedded server; not in the onboarding. |

Approved with the design:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Streamable HTTP without session, answered in JSON | Sessions, event streams | Claude asks nothing a session would hold (no sampling, no subscriptions); any api replica answers any request, and MCP's next version drops sessions. |
| The client's metadata document (CIMD) first, registration (DCR) for the other clients | Registration only | Claude's recommended setting; nothing to keep per client but a cache. |
| An authorization server of our own in `rewind-server`, on `sha2` and the seal of `/auth` | `oxide-auth` | Unmaintained since June 2024, and it knows neither metadata documents nor RFC 9728; the surface is small: codes with PKCE, refresh, registration, metadata documents. |
| A connection is a principal with grants (`connection:cn_…`), one per organization, of the built-in role `reader` or `harness` | A table of its own for its rights | Every rule of access applies as is: labels, no one giving more than they hold, the grant read at each request. |
| The agent tools move from `evals` to `rewind-agent-tools`, behind a port `Api`; `rewind-mcp-server` serves them | A third copy of the tools in the server | One Rust implementation of the SDK's tools, which the evals measure and Claude uses. |

### Taken for secrets and API toolsets (November 2026)

Decided by the owner on 19 and 20 November 2026, with the design reviewed the same days
([04](04-tools.md#api-tools), [authenticating calls](04-tools.md#authenticating-calls),
[06](06-storage-and-scale.md#what-the-caller-may-do), [07](07-code-architecture.md#http-api-v1)).

| Decision | Notes |
| --- | --- |
| **A toolset may call APIs described by OpenAPI** | The agent gives the document, and each operation is a tool: Rewind calls the customer's backend, which runs no worker. Host tools keep the same standing: a backend may pull its calls with a worker (long poll), as Dax does, and they serve what lives in the harness, Claude Code or a person's answer. The customer chooses, toolset by toolset. |
| **Rewind signs its calls with a key per tenant** | Ed25519, its public key read on a public endpoint. The customer's servers check who calls and for which end user, and Rewind keeps none of their secrets. |
| **A JWT per call** | Valid 60 s, with the namespace in `sub`, the audience, the run, the idempotency key and the SHA-256 of the body. |
| **A run reads only the secrets attached to it** | The agent attaches them when it writes the script, in the request, never in the script. It is the only way a secret is read, checked against the agent's rights, so who reads what is known. |
| **A saved workflow version is the permission** | Starting a run of a version needs no right on its secrets: the saver's were checked. A script written for one run, and a fork, are checked against their caller. |
| **Claude may attach secrets** | `secrets:use` is in the access "Run" of a connection, within the person's grant. |
| **The organization's secrets carry labels** | As runs do: a key limited to `env=prod` sees and attaches only the secrets labelled `env=prod`. |
| **The dashboard shows and edits secrets, and warns before deleting one** | Never a value. A deletion first shows the active runs and the workflows that attach the secret. |
| **One signing key per organization** | Asked on 20 November: a key per namespace adds nothing, since every private key would sit in the same store, read by the same workers, and `sub` comes from the run. One organization's environments are told apart by `labels`, which the server checks. |
| **The master key can be replaced** | Each data key records which master key encrypted it; the server reads with the current and the previous ones; a command encrypts the data keys again with the current one, without touching the secrets. |
| **The SDK checks Rewind's JWT** | `verifyRewindCall` does every check a receiving server must do, so a customer does not write them. |
| **Accepted: the workers that run scripts also read the secrets and sign** | In production on Railway, without gVisor, a script escaping V8 would reach the master key and every organization's signing key ([sandboxed workers](06-storage-and-scale.md#sandboxed-nodes)). |
| **Accepted: checking a JWT is the receiving server's job** | A server that checks the signature alone accepts a JWT replayed within its 60 s, on the same method and URL. |

Approved with the design:

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Named secrets in two scopes, the organization's (`org/<name>`) and a namespace's (`ns/<name>`, read in the run's namespace) | Credentials per toolset, per namespace and toolset, and per run, resolved with a fallback | A secret no longer depends on a toolset's digest, so a toolset registered again keeps its secrets; nothing falls back on a shared token that no one attached; an attachment names exactly what a run reads. |
| One string per secret; the server's `auth` says where it goes (a header and its format, a variable) | Free headers and variables per server | An attachment and the dashboard handle one value; its place is part of the pinned toolset, checked once. |
| No credentials per run | Keeping `PUT /v1/runs/{id}/credentials` | A namespace's secret covers the end user, and putting a secret again makes ready the parked calls of every run that attaches it. |
| `iss` is `{public-url}/organizations/{tenant}`, its keys at `{iss}/jwks.json` | One issuer for every tenant, with `org` as a claim | A server set up with one issuer cannot accept another tenant's JWT, even if it forgets a claim. |
| `jti` is `{run_id}/{seq}/{attempt}`, the idempotency key a claim of its own | `jti` equal to the idempotency key | A retried call is a new request with the same key: a server that refuses a `jti` it has seen would refuse the retry. |
| `labels`, `htm`, `htu` and `body` in the JWT | `sub` alone | Dax reads the run's labels (its goal) in its handlers; the method, the URL and the body bind a signature to one request. |
| A namespace's secret is attached by whoever starts a run in that namespace, without `secrets:use` | `secrets:use` for both scopes | It is the end user's own, and only their runs read it. |
| A grant or a token limited to labels never writes a namespace's secret (403) | Writing it within the namespace | A namespace's secret serves the end user's runs whatever their labels. |
| A secret never goes in a URL: an `apiKey` in the query or a cookie needs an explicit `auth` | Following the document | URLs end up in logs. |
| An API's default policy: `retry` for the idempotent methods, `at_most_once` for `POST` and `PATCH` unless the operation declares `Idempotency-Key` | `retry` everywhere, as for MCP | HTTP says which methods may be repeated; a `POST` without a key may act twice. |
| API tools live in `rewind-openapi`; the egress checks and the signing move to `rewind-outbound`, shared with `rewind-mcp` and the webhooks | API tools in `rewind-mcp` | Adapters never depend on each other, and the checks stay one implementation. |
| `--allow-internal-mcp` becomes `--allow-internal-tools` (`REWIND_ALLOW_INTERNAL_TOOLS`) | One option per kind of server | One rule for every server a tenant names. |
| Mock mode reads no secret and `/v1/test` checks no attachment | Checking them as a start does | A test calls nothing, and runs without the end user's secrets. |
