Messages from servers are cut at 2,000 characters | Retrying every error | Policies decide what a transient failure becomes; a tool error is the script's to handle. |
| The `http` tool's args are `{ url, method?, headers?: [[name, value]], body?, bodyEncoding?: "utf8" \| "base64" }` and its result `{ status, statusText, url, redirected, headers: [[name, value]], body, bodyEncoding? }` (base64 when the body is not UTF-8); `rewind_mcp::http_tool` pins them as schemas. Any status is a result; a refusal fails with `error`; a failure before anything was sent is transient, after it transient only for an idempotent method. `Host`, `Connection`, `Content-Length` and the other transport headers are refused. Redirects follow `fetch`: `303`, and `301`/`302` after a `POST`, become a `GET` without a body; credentials are dropped across origins. 5 MB is 5 MiB; 30 s is also bounded by the execution timeout | `fetch`'s full `Request` | A `POST` that may have taken effect must not be sent again; the runtime's `fetch` builds its `Response` from this result. |
| Egress is enforced by the HTTP client's only DNS resolver: it resolves once, refuses the name if any address is refused, and hands exactly those addresses to the connector; a URL whose host is an address is checked before the request. Refused: the IANA special-purpose ranges (private, loopback, link-local and metadata, CGNAT, documentation, benchmarking, multicast, reserved; IPv6 unique-local, link-local, site-local, Teredo, discard), IPv6 that embeds IPv4 judged by its IPv4. Allow-list entries are names, `*.domain` (its subdomains, not itself) or addresses, any port. No proxy setting: the egress proxy is a rule of the workers' network. The range table is ours | Checking, then letting the client resolve again; a connector of our own | A second resolution is the DNS-rebinding hole; the resolver hook gives the guarantee by construction. `IpAddr::is_global` is unstable and no maintained crate classifies addresses. |
| `rmcp` 3.9 for the client; tests run fixture servers built with `rmcp` over HTTP and stdio. The integration test binary uses libtest-mimic, so that it can also be the stdio server it starts; a test against a reference server runs when `REWIND_MCP_REFERENCE_SERVER` names one (passed against `@modelcontextprotocol/server-everything`) | A server binary in the crate; a Node fixture | No extra target in normal builds, no Node in Rust tests. |

### Taken while integrating the tools (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| One format for the `http` tool, `rewind-mcp`'s: the glue's `fetch` writes its args (a text body, or base64 with `"bodyEncoding": "base64"`) and reads its result. Its schemas exist once, in `rewind_mcp::http_tool`; `rewind_runtime::builtins` keeps only the `webhook` schema. The corpus pins `rewind_mcp::http_tool`, so the op checks every request `fetch` builds against it, and a scenario's `http` responses must match its output schema | The runtime's `body: {"text": …} \| {"base64": …}`; a copy of the schema in each crate | `rewind-mcp` implements the tool, and a flat text body is what people write in mocks and read in journals. The runtime cannot depend on `rewind-mcp`: one copy, checked in the corpus, cannot drift. |
| The glue gives the result of an MCP tool pinned without an output schema the `text()` and `json()` of `ToolContent`: `rewind:tools` calls such a tool through `callToolContent`, and the delivered value gets a frozen prototype holding both helpers, not enumerable. A result that is not an object (a mock's) is delivered as it is | Own properties; adding the helpers in the dispatcher | JSON carries no functions. The value's own keys stay the result's: the journal, `JSON.stringify` and replay see the same value. |
| A call without arguments sends `{}`, recorded and checked against the pinned schema; `null` stays `null` and is checked as it is | `null` also sent as `{}` | The journal records what the script passed; `args?` does not admit `null` at the check. |
| The checker's `tsconfig.json` sets `verbatimModuleSyntax`: the types of `rewind:tools` (`ToolContent`, `ContentBlock`, a namespace's interfaces) are imported with `import type` | Letting the runtime accept type names it does not export | The runtime refuses at start a name `rewind:tools` does not export. Without the option tsgo accepted `import { ToolContent }`, and the run was refused after its check had passed. |
| The contract between codegen, the checker and the runtime is tested in `rewind-e2e` (`tests/declarations.rs`): codegen's declarations of a pinned toolset, checked with `rewind-check`, accept what the runtime starts and refuse args of the wrong type, `fetch` without the `http` tool, built-ins imported from `rewind:tools` and types imported as values; every golden `rewind-tools.d.ts` of `rewind-codegen` checks. Scenarios gain `mcp_servers` and `output_schemas` | A dev-dependency of `rewind-check` on `rewind-codegen` | The contract spans three crates, and only a tooling crate may depend on all of them. |

### Taken while building OpenTelemetry (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `rewind-otel` on `opentelemetry` 0.33: OTLP over gRPC (tonic) or HTTP with protobuf (async reqwest), both with TLS; the transport is configured, else `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL`, else HTTP, OpenTelemetry's default; the other `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_*` variables apply. An `OtlpConfig` endpoint is the collector's base URL; over HTTP `/v1/traces` and `/v1/logs` are appended | One transport | Collectors and vendors take both; some take only HTTP. |
| `OtelSpanExporter` calls the OTLP exporters directly, one export per call, and returns their answer; it keeps the projector's ids, parents, millisecond times, statuses and attributes, under the scope `rewind.run`; a log's observed time is its journal time | The SDK's batch processors | A failed export must leave the feed item claimed, so the projector needs the answer; it already batches by feed item. A record exported again is the same record. |
| Service telemetry goes through `rewind-otel`'s own `tracing` layer: it builds the SDK's `SpanData` when a span closes and hands it to the SDK's batch processor; events become log records through the SDK's logger, correlated with the span they happen in | `tracing-opentelemetry` and `opentelemetry-appender-tracing` | An activation's span id is `H(run, fence)`, and its trace is known only once it has read its run. `tracing-opentelemetry` 0.34 and the SDK 0.33 cannot give a span an id of our choosing, nor move it to another trace once created. |
| A `tracing` span joins a trace through the fields `trace_id`, `span_id`, `parent_span_id` (hex) or `traceparent` (W3C), recorded at creation or later; without them it belongs to the trace of the span it is nested in, under it, or starts its own. Ids are looked up when a span closes | OpenTelemetry context in the engine | The engine and the adapters never name OpenTelemetry, and the dispatcher already holds the tool call's `traceparent`. The spans nested in an activation follow the identity it records late. |
| A service span is exported when its trace is sampled, decided from the trace id as for run telemetry; service logs are always exported, with the sampled flag of their trace. An `ERROR` event marks its span as failed | Sampling service logs | Every worker and the projector agree on a run. Logs report the platform's health, whatever a run's sampling. |
| Service exports run as tasks on the Tokio runtime the pipeline was built in, with telemetry suppressed; the targets of the export path (`opentelemetry`, `h2`, `hyper`, `hyper_util`, `reqwest`, `rustls`, `tonic`, `tower`) are never exported. `install` returns a guard whose drop exports what is buffered | The SDK's processors on an async runtime (experimental); a blocking HTTP client | One async HTTP client serves both kinds of telemetry, and an export never causes another. |
| `SpanExporter` has a conformance suite (`conformance::spans`). The port has no read side, so the suite reads the backend through a `Collector` the adapter's test provides: the memory exporter is its own; `rewind-otel` passes the suite on the SDK's in-memory exporters, and on an OpenTelemetry Collector in Docker (`otel/opentelemetry-collector`, its file exporter) over gRPC and HTTP, skipped only when Docker is absent | Testing the adapters alone | Every port with interchangeable adapters has a suite. Reading the backend makes the contract observable: ids, parents, times, attributes, logs, and a span exported twice being the same span. |

### Taken while building the production store (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `rewind-store-postgres` uses sqlx 0.8 (Postgres, tokio, rustls on aws-lc-rs), with queries built at run time | sqlx 0.9; tokio-postgres with a pool crate; sqlx's compile-time checked queries | sqlx 0.9 needs Rust 1.94, above the workspace's 1.88. sqlx brings the pool and the migrator. Checked queries need a database or an offline cache at build time. aws-lc-rs is the provider the HTTP clients already use. |
| The schema is migrated by sqlx's migrator, its migrations embedded in the binary, recorded with their checksums in `_sqlx_migrations`, under the migrator's advisory lock. A database with a migration this build does not know is refused (`Error::NewerSchema`), and so is a migration changed since it was applied | The schema version in `meta`, as the embedded store keeps it | Several servers start together on one database and must migrate it once. `meta` serves the embedded server (virtual time), which Postgres never backs. |
| Times and durations are `bigint` milliseconds; digests `bytea`; run ids `uuid`; labels `jsonb`, filtered in SQL (`@>`, a GIN index); events and payload values `text`, exactly as `rewind-format` and RFC 8785 write them; text that is compared or ordered uses the `"C"` collation | `timestamptz`; `jsonb` events | The domain's arithmetic and saturation, unchanged. `jsonb` would rewrite the stored bytes of an event. Tenants must be in byte order whatever the database's collation: the draw of `acquire_runnable` indexes them. |
| `runs` is keyed by `(tenant, id)`, with `id` unique; `history` by `(run_id, idx)` and `inbox` by `(run_id, id)`, its ids from a sequence; a tool task keeps its run's tenant and toolset. A test runs the journal's conformance suite again with `history` and `inbox` partitioned by hash of `run_id` | Keying `runs` by `id`, as the embedded store does | The tenant starts every key (06). A partitioned table's keys must hold its partition key, and identity columns only work on partitioned tables from Postgres 17. Claims filter tenants and toolsets without a join. |
| Transactions run at `READ COMMITTED`. Rows workers compete for are picked with `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` (runnable, quarantined and expired runs, due tool tasks, feed items). Everything a run owns is written under its row lock, taken first; several runs are locked by id, payload rows by digest. A deadlock or a serialization failure reruns the transaction, 8 times at most | `SERIALIZABLE` | The run lock orders an enqueue with the commit that consumes the inbox or ends the run: no wake-up is lost and nothing is enqueued for an ended run. Fixed lock orders leave no cycle; `SERIALIZABLE` would abort transactions on the hottest rows. |
| A claim decides on a concurrency limit only under a transaction-scoped advisory lock on it, per run and tool and per worker, tenant and MCP server, taken with `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock` before counting the calls in flight; a slot another claim holds is skipped | Locking the run's row in claims | Two claimers would otherwise both count the same free slot. A try-lock never waits, so claims never block commits or each other. |
| `acquire_runnable` leases in the statement that picks: its sticky runs first, then the tenants with eligible runs, read one index probe each (a recursive skip scan), the drawn tenant's oldest run, and the next tenant when concurrent workers hold all of the drawn one's | `SELECT DISTINCT` over the runnable runs; giving up on the drawn tenant | The draw stays the port's (`draw` modulo the tenants, by id), and a worker never goes idle while another tenant has work. |
| `create_run` locks the tenant's settings row while it counts the active runs | Counting without a lock | Concurrent starts would each see the last free slot of the quota. |
| Postgres keeps its durability defaults (`synchronous_commit = on`): a commit is on disk when it returns. Measured with `postgres:17` in Docker on the shared 8-core laptop, each activation with its tool task claimed and completed (`cargo run --release -p rewind-store-postgres --example postgres_load`): from one worker, 158 activations per second, `commit_activation` p50 2.0 ms and p99 3.6 ms; 389 per second from 4 workers of one engine, 565 from 2 engines × 4, 829 from 4 × 4. Under a heavier load of the machine, 23 to 46 per second from one worker and about 670 from 4 × 4: Docker's disk flushes bound one worker, and concurrent commits share their flushes | Asynchronous commits everywhere | The journal is the only source of truth. Throughput grows with workers and engines, as the scaling path wants. |
| `release`, `renew` and `forget_sticky` commit without waiting for the WAL to reach the disk (`synchronous_commit` off for the statement) | Waiting, as every other write does | Losing them in a crash of the database only makes a lease end later or sooner, or keeps a sticky priority; the fence still guards every commit, and any durable commit after them flushes them too. A release went from 3–4 ms to 0.5–1 ms in Docker on a laptop. |
| The store repeats the embedded store's encodings (routes, shapes, labels, triggers) and its envelope encryption of secrets; two servers storing a tenant's first secret at once keep the first data key stored | A crate shared by both stores | Adapters never depend on each other, and a value must read the same whatever the backend. |
| The tests run `postgres:17` in Docker: one container per test binary, with a unique name, a random local port and its data in a tmpfs, removed at the end; each conformance check gets a new database. They are skipped only when Docker is absent. A concurrency test drives 60 runs from 3 engines × 4 workers on one database, with stalled workers and signals sent twice | testcontainers | The same harness as the S3 tests, without another dependency tree. |

| A subscription's id is `sub_` and a UUID v7, from which its `createdAt` is read | A column for the time | The port's `Subscription` has none, and the id already holds it. |
| `GET /v1/runs` reads the read model, newest first, and completes each summary with the run's record (quarantine, parent, status, the error that ended it); the filters on an error name, on quarantine and on the parent are applied after, scanning at most 2,000 summaries per page, the cursor being the last run scanned. A run is listed once its first activation is projected. `GET /v1/scripts` sorts by last run in the server, the cursor being `{ms}.{digest}`. Tool statistics count the calls scheduled in the window (the doc of `ToolStatsFilter` said completed) | New read model queries | The read model has no quarantine, parent or error name; the pages stay correct with filters applied after. |
| `GET /v1/runs/{id}/journal` exports a self-contained document (every payload's value), read from the archive once the run is archived; the cassette's matching is `sequential` | The archive as it is | An archive holds large payloads by digest (08), and `rewind replay` of an export must work without the server. |
| OTLP is on when `--otlp-endpoint` is given or `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` (or `…_TRACES_ENDPOINT`) is set; then `rewind-otel` exports service telemetry and run telemetry, else the projector applies the `otel` feed and drops it | Always exporting | A local server has no collector. |

### Taken while finishing service telemetry (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Measurements go through `tracing`. On an event, a field `monotonic_counter.<name>`, `counter.<name>` (up-down), `histogram.<name>` or `gauge.<name>` records its value, as `tracing-opentelemetry` names them; on a span, a field `histogram.<name>` records the span's duration in seconds when it closes. Only the fields named `rewind.*` are attributes. `rewind-otel`'s layer records them with the SDK's instruments, created by name at first use. The instruments are listed in `rewind_engine::metrics` | The `metrics` crate's facade; OpenTelemetry instruments in the engine | The engine and the adapters keep one facade and never name OpenTelemetry, and the prefixes are an existing convention. A run id logged next to a measurement never multiplies its series. |
| Durations are the durations of spans, timed by the subscriber: `rewind.activation.duration` (lease to commit or failure), `rewind.replay.duration`, `rewind.tool_attempt.duration`. The engine reads no clock to measure | `Instant` in the engine; the `Clock` port | Non-determinism enters the engine only through ports (principle 6), and the `Clock` counts milliseconds and stands still in virtual time. |
| A measurement is not a log: it is never exported as a log record nor written to stderr. `RUST_LOG` filters spans and logs only: each layer of the subscriber has its own filter | One filter for the whole subscriber | Metrics must not disappear when an operator lowers the log level. |
| Run metrics (`rewind.runs.started`, `rewind.runs.ended` by outcome, `rewind.tool_call.duration` in the run's logical time, by tool and outcome) are derived from committed entries by the projector's `otel` consumer, and emitted once the feed item is acknowledged. They are never sampled; mock runs count only when their telemetry is exported | Counting at commit in the engine; a method for metrics on `SpanExporter` | Metrics about runs are projections of the journal, like the dashboard's tool statistics. A counter is not idempotent: emitted after the acknowledgment, an item applied again after a failure is not counted twice (a crash between the two loses its measurements instead). The port stays as approved. |
| Service metrics: activations by outcome (`lease_lost` is lease contention), the inbox depth as the triggers each activation takes, replays, sticky cache lookups (`hit`, `miss`) and evictions, quarantines by kind, tool attempts by outcome, and retries after a transient failure | A gauge of all inboxes' depth; counting lease attempts | No port can count inboxes, and the ports stay as approved. With `SKIP LOCKED` a worker never waits for a lease: contention shows only when a lease is taken over. |
| The engine opens the tool attempt span around each dispatch, with the call's `traceparent`: a child of the tool call span, whatever the dispatcher | A span in each dispatcher | MCP, built-ins and mocks get the same span; the engine already holds the trace context it forwards. |
| Metrics are exported by the SDK's periodic reader (`OTEL_METRIC_EXPORT_INTERVAL`, 60 s by default), cumulative unless `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_TEMPORALITY_PREFERENCE` says otherwise, over the transport of traces and logs (`/v1/metrics` over HTTP). The reader lends each batch for the export's duration, so its own thread drives the export on the Tokio runtime instead of spawning a task. Histograms of durations have buckets from 1 ms to a day; the others, powers of two up to 1024 | The reader on an async runtime (experimental) | One runtime and one HTTP client serve every export. A host tool can wait a day for a person. |

A failed expectation exits 1 | A format of the CLI's own; a project-wide `rewind.tools.json` | One shape for the file and the API. A server's toolset always pins `webhook`, so a scenario's does. |
| `rewind replay` always runs in the CLI process: a run id is exported by the server first. Matching exits 0, diverging 1 | A compatibility endpoint on the server | The server needs no V8 for it, and an exported file and a run give the same report. |
| Exit codes of errors: a refusal of the server (404, 409, 410, 429, 401, 403) 6; an invalid request, toolset, media type or size, and a file the command cannot read 64; `invalid-script` 65; a 5xx or an unreachable server 70. A command that waits exits with what became of the run; one that does not (`cancel` without `--wait`, `signal`, `release`) exits 0 once done | Mapping only 404 and 409 | Each outcome a script can act on has its own code. |
| `--wait` (30 s by default for `run` and `fork`) is a deadline counted from before the start. A quarantined run stops the wait (5) | Waiting until the run ends; waiting in the start request | A durable run may sleep for days; quarantine needs a person. The lock is never checked while its own server starts; three servers at most per command, 30 s to answer | Holding the lock in the CLI; spawning without checking | A check at the wrong moment makes a starting server miss the lock: retrying settles it. A server started from a terminal must survive its Ctrl-C. |

The command line is `clap` (derive, with `REWIND_DIR`, `REWIND_URL`, `REWIND_API_KEY`) | Logs at `info`; a parser of our own | A CLI is quiet; local commands still report through the same telemetry as a server. |

The end-to-end tests through the `rewind` binary live in `crates/rewind/tests/cli.rs` | Tests through the binary only | Each layer fails on its own, and the binary is only needed for what spans processes. |

The call whose `ToolError` failed a run is found by `ScriptError::failed_call`, as the server finds it; a signal or a cancellation is processed at once, at the current virtual time, as the server's workers would | Texts of their own | The evals measure what a harness gives its model through the SDK and the server. |

The world answers a signal wait at most once per run and signal name | Checking at the end of the conversation | A durable task (sleep 3 days, wait for an approval) is done when its run is, not when the model stops talking. |

OpenAI: stateless (`store: false`), reasoning items sent back with their encrypted content. | Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |

| A deployed server sends lifecycle webhooks only to `https://` URLs whose addresses are public, with the egress checks of the `http` tool: when a subscription is created (its name resolved then) and at each delivery, where the client's only resolver resolves once, refuses a name with any refused address and hands the checked addresses to the connector, without proxies; no redirect is followed. The checks stay in `rewind-mcp`, exported as `rewind_mcp::Egress` (`rewind-server` is a composition root, which may depend on an adapter). The embedded server (one local tenant) and a server started with `--allow-private-webhooks` (an operator's choice, on a trusted network) send to any `http://` or `https://` URL. This replaces "or `http://` toward the machine itself" in the row on outbound webhooks | Keeping `http://localhost` on deployed servers; a crate of its own for the checks | The machine of a deployed server is inside its network: a tenant's subscription reached internal services and the cloud metadata endpoint. The range table and the guard against DNS rebinding exist once. A harness on the same private network needs the operator's consent. |
| Lifecycle webhooks are sent concurrently, at most 4 at once per subscription. A delivery has a window of twice the sender's retry window (2 × 135 s): it waits for a slot, is sent with the retries cut at the window's end, and is given up and logged when the window ends. The worker claims `webhooks` feed items for that window plus 30 s, up to 256 at once, and acknowledges each item once its deliveries end; an item claimed again while its deliveries are in progress (virtual time) is not sent twice. The engine's sender (`run.quarantined`, `tool.schema_changed`, `credentials.expired`) queues the delivery and returns; the delivery runs in the background, within its window, and is dropped at a stop. This supersedes the stop of the row on the worker role's webhook loop: the deliveries in progress are left unacknowledged and sent again, with the same `webhook-id`, once their claims expire | One item after the other under the projector's 30 s claim; renewing feed claims (a new store method); a queue of deliveries in the store | A receiver that was down held up every tenant's webhooks, and a delivery's retries outlasted its claim, so a second worker sent it again; an activation waited for `run.quarantined`. A claim sized to the window keeps one sender per item without changing the stores. |
| A host-tool failure (`POST /v1/tasks/{token}/fail`, also for a deferred task) holds at most 64 KiB: its message and its data as canonical JSON, together; a rejection's message too. Above, `payload-too-large` (413). The SDK's worker then sends the failure again without its data, its message cut at 2,000 characters | The 8 MiB of results; no bound | A failure stays in its journal entry: unlike a result, it never moves to the payloads table or the blob store, so it is bounded by the largest value a row holds next to a digest (64 KiB, the table tier of 06). 2,000 characters is where `rewind-mcp` cuts a server's message. |
| A key limited to labels reads a script only once a run it sees ran it (the read model's runs), and a payload only through a run it sees whose journal holds it: `GET /v1/payloads/{digest}?run=<id>`, `run` required for such a key (403 without) and checked for any key that gives it (404). It sets the credentials of its own runs, never a toolset's: `PUT /v1/toolsets/{id}/credentials` and `POST /v1/toolsets` with `credentials` answer 403. The SDK's agent tools and approvals and the dashboard read payloads through their run | Scanning the runs a key sees for the digest; a store method listing the runs that reference a payload | A key limited to labels is one conversation's: a digest learned elsewhere, or guessed, must not open another conversation's data, and a toolset's credentials serve every run of the tenant. A caller always knows the run a payload came from. |

### Taken while reviewing the sandbox and the checker (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| What the script emits reacting to one trigger is bounded in the sandbox: 50,000 commands and annotations, and 64 MiB of the text they carry (log messages, signal names, tool arguments and the output as JSON, the error of a failure), `SandboxLimits::emitted_per_trigger` and `emitted_bytes_per_trigger`. Past either bound the op drops what it was given and terminates the execution, and the call ends with `LimitExceeded`: the run is aborted `limit_exceeded`. A string longer than what is left is refused before it is copied out of V8, and the arguments and the output are counted before they are parsed | Leaving it to the engine's limits per activation | The engine checks its limits once the call has returned, and what the script emitted lives outside V8's heap limit until then: a loop of `console.log` held 1 GiB after 0.3 s under a 128 MiB heap, and a worker that ran out of memory left the run to the next one. 50,000 is the default cap on a history: a response above it could never be committed. A string built by concatenation can be far longer than the heap (2^27 characters, 384 MiB as UTF-8), and parsed JSON takes several times its text. |
| A check returns at most 1,000 diagnostics, the first by position (`TsgoChecker::MAX_DIAGNOSTICS`); the rules stop at as many findings, and at most 4 MiB of tsgo's output is read, tsgo being stopped past it | Every diagnostic | A script reaches 16 MiB through the API, and one with an error on each line gave 200,000 diagnostics from 1.3 MiB, after 72 s and with a memory use growing with them. A model fixes the first errors first. |
| V8 runs with `--single-threaded`: it compiles and collects garbage on the isolate's thread only | V8's background threads (concurrent compilation, marking and sweeping) | Optimized code was installed at a time that varied between two runs of the same script, and the size of its frames with it: the same script caught its first stack overflow at a depth of 8880, 9319 or 9503, and its replay diverged. Single-threaded, the depth is the same on every run of a build. It still depends on the build, because V8 sets the stack limit from where the isolate was created; pinning it needs `CreateParams::set_stack_limit`, which is unsafe. A flag of V8 is part of the epoch, like V8 itself. |

### Taken while fixing the review of the engine and the stores (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| No commit references a deleted blob. The engine reserves the rows of the payloads of the blob tier before writing their blobs (`reserve_payloads`: a missing row is created, a row without references starts its grace again). The cleanup marks the rows it deletes (`payloads.deletion_started_at`, `forget_payloads`), deletes their blobs, then removes the rows still marked at that time (`drop_payloads`). A commit refuses a payload held by digest whose row is missing or marked (`Conflict`); a payload whose deletion has started is committed with its value in its row, which stops the deletion; a deletion not finished within `PAYLOAD_DELETION_TIMEOUT` (1 h) is started again | The commit touching the row and the cleanup checking it again after deleting the row; failing the activation until the deletion ends | A blob delete cannot be fenced: whatever the cleanup checks first, its delete can reach the blob store after a writer's put. Only the writer can avoid counting on the blob, if it learns that a deletion has started before it writes. Keeping the value in its row costs one large row in a rare race, where waiting would fail activations and could quarantine a healthy run. A blob written by a worker that died before its commit now has a row, and is deleted after the grace. Postgres locks the payload rows of a commit or a reservation by digest before writing them. |
| `runs_to_archive` and `archive_run` skip a finished run that still has feed items | Archiving, and the projector reading the archive | The projector reads the run's whole history to apply an item: the read model, the traces and the webhooks would miss the run's last entries. |
| The schemas of Turso and Postgres stay at version 1, changed in place: `payloads.deletion_started_at` | A migration to version 2 | No database was written by a release. |
| Text the stores keep never holds a NUL character (U+0000). The engine refuses it at its entry points, before anything is stored: the script, labels, the idempotency keys of runs and signals, the trace context and the toolset's declarations (`StartError::NulCharacter`, `EngineError::NulCharacter`, answered `400 invalid_request`), and the name given to the `webhook` built-in (a `ToolError` of kind `error`). The server refuses it in what it writes itself: a toolset's declarations, a subscription's URL and labels, a key's label scopes. Values keep it: they are JSON, where it is escaped. A quarantine reason, which may quote a script's text, gets U+FFFD instead | Escaping it in the Postgres store; refusing it in Postgres only | Postgres refuses U+0000 in `text` and `jsonb` where Turso and memory keep it: refused at the way in, every store behaves alike and the caller learns why instead of getting a 500. Escaping would change what is read back, and the script's digest. Tenants, tool names and server names already refuse control characters. |

The bounds change no declaration a model could use. |
| Service telemetry never exports the spans and events of `rmcp`; they stay on stderr | Exporting them as other dependencies' | `rmcp` logs the MCP messages it carries: a server's notifications at `info`, every call's arguments and result at `debug` and `trace`. | The key of a run an agent tool starts (`run_script`, `retry_run`) is the SHA-256 of `[labels, toolCallId]` in compact JSON, the labels as `[key, value]` pairs sorted by key (`runIdempotencyKey`, and `run_key` in the evals); `signal_run` keeps the tool call id, a signal's key being unique per run. The check of the run answered stays, as a safety net. This changes the decision that the tool call id is the key | The tool call id itself | A run's key is unique per tenant, and harnesses number their tool calls alike (`call_0`, `call_1`…): two agents collided, and the SDK threw. Agents with other labels can no longer collide. |
| The evals' agent tools bound their texts as the SDK does: messages and details, names and hints, diagnostics within the trace's room, test failures, then the whole result | Bounding only the output and the trace | The evals measure what a model reads through the SDK. |

### Taken while fixing the runs that kill their workers and the lookups with a NUL (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| An activation whose worker dies counts as a failed one. `acquire_runnable` counts the activations started since the run's last commit (`runs.attempts`, incremented in the statement that leases), and a commit and `release_quarantine` reset it; `acquire_run` and `acquire_quarantined` do not count. When the engine leases a run whose last 5 activations committed nothing, it quarantines it (kind `failures`, the reason saying how many never finished and how many failed) instead of running its script; `quarantine` takes the kind | Counting in `record_failure` only; a registry of live workers; the store quarantining the run by itself when it leases it | A worker killed by a script (a V8 fatal error, the container killed for its memory, a bug in a native dependency) never calls `record_failure`: its run went from worker to worker and killed each, forever. Only the store sees every lease, and counting there costs nothing more. The engine writes the reason and reports the quarantine as it reports the others (`run.quarantined`, metrics). A run activated in the same process when it dies is counted too; it is quarantined only if that happens 5 times in a row without a commit between, and can be released. `runs.attempts` is added to the schemas in place, at version 1. |
| Only a commit makes a worker sticky, and the grace counts from the oldest inbox item: a worker that dies during an activation holds the run until its lease expires, and not longer. The conformance suite and an engine test pin it | Clearing the sticky worker when another worker leases the run | The trigger the dead worker took has waited longer than the 5 s grace when its lease expires, so the priority adds nothing. A live sticky worker keeps its cached instance, still valid while nothing is committed. |
| A key looked up with a NUL character matches nothing, and is not sent to the store: a webhook token (404, in the server), a task token (`TaskGone`, 410, in the engine's use cases), and an id that does not parse (404) are not found; a label or tool filter (`GET /v1/runs`, `GET /v1/scripts`, `GET /v1/tasks`) is refused with 400 `invalid_request`, by the server | Answering an empty list for a filter; escaping the character in Postgres; a check in each store | No stored text holds the character, and Postgres refuses it in a query: the lookup failed with 503 "the store cannot be reached", which a client retries for nothing. A filter that can match nothing is a mistake of the caller, told as one. |

An operator lets internal MCP servers through with `--allow-internal-mcp` (`REWIND_ALLOW_INTERNAL_MCP`), repeated or comma-separated: host names, `*.domain` (its subdomains), addresses and CIDR networks (`ipnet`); a name lets through every address it resolves to, an address or a network any name resolving into it. The embedded server, the CLI and the evals reach any address (`McpEgress::open`); `McpConfig`'s default is the checks without exceptions. A refused address fails the registration (`toolset-unavailable`) and the call (`unavailable`), for good. Decided by the owner's delegate | A switch for the whole private network, as `--allow-private-webhooks`; leaving it to the workers' egress proxy | A tenant's toolset made the API role and the workers reach internal services and the cloud metadata endpoint, and read them back through discovery errors. The API role has no egress proxy. A list names what the operator trusts, where a switch would open every internal service to every tenant. |
| The MCP client follows a redirect only within the server's origin, with a 307 or a 308, up to 5 times; the resolver checks each new connection. Another redirect fails the attempt for good, its reason never quoting the location. The embedded server follows the same rule | `rmcp`'s default client (the same origin, 10 hops, other redirects returned as responses); following redirects to other origins, checked again | The credentials are custom headers, which a client carries to any origin a redirect leads to. A 301, 302 or 303 turns the `POST` of a message into a `GET` without it. |
| A connection is internal once its client reached an address only the operator's list lets through, noted by the resolver at each connection, or given in the URL. An internal connection's failures quote neither a body, a challenge nor a parse error of what the server answered: only the HTTP status and the client's own errors (no connection, a timeout). The JSON-RPC errors of an open session, what an MCP server says to its clients, are quoted as for any server. Public servers, stdio servers and the embedded server's keep the server's text, cut at 2,000 characters | Withholding every server's text on a deployed server; deciding from the URL before connecting | A tenant could read an internal service through the errors of a toolset that points at a page of it. A developer needs a public server's message. A tenant's name can resolve into an allowed network only when the connection is made. |
| A server name, a host-tool group and a host tool without a group are refused when they are a reserved word of ECMAScript 2025, a word strict mode reserves (`implements`, `interface`, `let`, `package`, `private`, `protected`, `public`, `static`), or `eval` and `arguments`, which strict mode does not let a declaration take (`rewind_core::RESERVED_WORDS`). Contextual keywords and global names (`async`, `of`, `type`, `undefined`, `globalThis`…) are accepted: the checker and the runtime take them, which `rewind-e2e`'s contract test checks | Leaving such a host tool out of `rewind:tools`, as one whose name is no identifier; renaming it | `rewind:tools` declares these names (`export const delete`), and a module is strict mode code: the declarations failed to parse, and the runtime's module too. A refusal at registration says why; a tool left out would vanish from the declarations without a word. |

### Taken while bounding the sandbox's memory (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `unsafe` code lives in one module, `rewind-runtime`'s `memory`: the allocator of array buffers, its hook on collections, and the stack limit. The crate denies `unsafe_code` instead of forbidding it, `memory` allows it, every block carries a `// SAFETY:` comment (`clippy::undocumented_unsafe_blocks` is denied there), and `rewind-architecture-tests` refuses `unsafe`, or a relaxed lint, anywhere else. Allowed by the owner's delegate | No `unsafe` anywhere | rusty_v8 offers these only through raw pointers (`new_rust_allocator`, collection callbacks with data), and does not bind `Isolate::SetStackLimit`. |
| Each instance's array buffers come from an allocator bounded by `SandboxLimits::array_buffer_bytes`, 128 MiB by default (the heap limit's default). Past it the allocator returns null: V8 collects all garbage, asks again, then throws `RangeError: Array buffer allocation failed`, which the script can catch | Ending the call with `LimitExceeded`; V8's own allocator | V8 kept array buffers outside the heap limit: `new ArrayBuffer(2 ** 36)` aborted the process (V8 checks that one buffer changes its accounting by less than 32 GiB), and 1.5 GiB of typed arrays fitted under a 64 MiB heap. Only V8 knows its last attempt, and it reports it as a `RangeError`. The error is deterministic: V8 runs single-threaded and scans the stack precisely (no direct handles in this build), so after its last-resort collection the allocator holds exactly the buffers the script can reach, the same live and on replay. |
| The near-heap-limit callback ignores the call V8 makes at the start of the collection it runs before giving up on an array buffer: the allocator records its refusal, a collection prologue forgets it, and the callback then neither terminates nor raises the limit | Terminating on every call | V8 makes that call whatever the heap's size: the script ended with "more than N MiB of heap" instead of its `RangeError`. |
| A resizable `ArrayBuffer` takes its `maxByteLength` from the budget when it is created, through a fixed buffer of that length that a `WeakMap` keeps as long as the resizable one, allocated before V8 reserves anything. The glue wraps the global `ArrayBuffer`, as it wraps `Date`; `transfer` moves the reservation, `transferToFixedLength` releases it, and `structuredClone` reserves one for each resizable buffer of its result (walked only once the instance created one) | Refusing `maxByteLength`; not counting them | V8 reserves their pages itself, outside the allocator: four buffers of 2^36 bytes were accepted, and creating one with that length aborted the process (the same 32 GiB check). V8 creates a resizable buffer only through the constructor, `transfer` and its deserializer. The checker declares them, and what type-checks is what exists. No journal of the corpus changed. |
| Once the heap limit is really reached, the callback terminates the execution and raises the limit by 1 GiB + 64 MiB at each call: room for V8's largest object (2^27 elements, or 2^29 two-byte characters) | Doubling the limit at each call; a ceiling | The termination takes effect at V8's next interrupt check, and V8 aborts the process when the raised limit leaves no room for the allocation in progress: doubling once was not enough for one 256 MiB string (`"ab".repeat(2 ** 27).toUpperCase()`, the spread or a regular expression's split of a 2^28-character string). Interruptible code stops at once, so the heap grows by what V8 was allocating, not by the raise: those now end at 140 to 450 MiB. A ceiling would only turn a built-in that runs on into an abort of the process. |
| The glue measures, before they start, the built-ins that V8 runs to their end creating elements over a length: `Array.prototype.fill`, `toReversed`, `toSorted`, `toSpliced`, `with`, `Array.from` of an array-like without a mapping function, and `String.prototype.split` with a string separator. One that must create more than min(heap limit / 8, 2^27) elements ends the call as the heap limit does (`op_rewind_heap_exceeded`, then `run_aborted { limit_exceeded }` with the heap's message). It reads only what runs no script code: the length of an array that is not a proxy or an own data property `length`, and primitive arguments; anything else goes to V8 unchecked | Letting V8 run them; a `RangeError` | `new Array(2 ** 27).fill(1.5)` ran 18 s and took 1.9 GiB under a 64 MiB heap (the review measured 10.5 s and 2.3 GB); `toReversed`, `toSorted`, `toSpliced`, `with` and `Array.from({ length })` alike; `"x,".repeat(2 ** 27).split(",")` made V8 abort the process ("invalid size"). An element takes at least 8 bytes of heap, so such a call can only exceed the heap; it now ends in 90 ms under 45 MiB. The bound depends on the deployment's heap limit, so it is a host abort, never an error the script could catch. |
| What V8 still runs to its end is left to the worker's container (measured under a 64 MiB heap, debug build): `JSON.stringify` of a sparse array of 2^26 elements builds 1.6 GiB outside the heap; `Object.entries` of a 2^28-character string reaches 0.8 GiB before the heap limit ends it; a global regular expression's `match` with 2^28 results aborts the process ("invalid size"); `indexOf`, `includes`, `reverse` and `join` over an array-like of length 2^32 or more run past 60 s, out of reach of the CPU budget | Reimplementing every generic built-in in the glue | Each needs its own exact reimplementation, for scripts that build strings or lengths of hundreds of millions. The container's memory limit bounds the memory (the reference deployment's `deploy/compose.yaml` sets `mem_limit`, 1 GiB by default); it does not bound the CPU of an activation stuck in V8. |
| The stack limit is set again before every call into the script, 984 KiB (V8's default `--stack-size`) below the calling frame, through `v8::Isolate::SetStackLimit` (its C++ symbol, declared in `memory`), and the isolate threads have a 4 MiB stack | V8's limit, set once; a fixed `--stack-size` and thread size alone | V8 sets its limit from the top of the thread, so every Rust frame between the thread's start and the call counted: a recursion overflowed at a depth of 14,743 in top-level code and 8,883 in `main` in a debug build, 15,130 and 8,939 in a release build. Pinned: 15,308 and 8,981 against 15,385 and 8,985. What is left is the few frames of rusty_v8 and deno_core between the call and V8, compiled differently in a debug build; a change of Rewind's own code no longer moves the depth, a new Rust compiler may move it by a few frames, as a new V8 does. |
| Before it takes the snapshot of an epoch, the runtime gives deno_core's JavaScript arrays of V8's own for its tick, immediate and timer information (`Deno.core.__setTickInfo`, `__setImmediateInfo`, `__setTimerInfo`); an isolate started from the snapshot gets buffers over its own state again. A test runs the lifecycle of isolates under Guard Malloc on macOS (`tests/stress.rs`), and fails without it | Patching deno_core; building the snapshot in another process | The `rewind` binary aborted now and then while V8 disposed an isolate: V8 freed one of its own array buffers ("pointer being freed was not allocated"; the stack goes through the allocator, with V8's default one as with the capped one). Under Guard Malloc every process faulted earlier, at its first instance: deno_core's `JsRuntimeForSnapshot::snapshot` frees its context state, whose memory JavaScript holds as those three array buffers, before V8 serializes the context, which reads them. With the change, the server's and the CLI's suites, the runtime's tests and the corpus run clean under Guard Malloc. Ruled out: the watchdog (joined before the isolate goes) and a concurrent sweep (single-threaded, V8 sweeps array buffers on the isolate's thread). The abort itself was not reproduced on demand (about one suite in 24 under the load of other builds, then none in 216, with or without the change), so its link to this read of freed memory is likely, not proven. |

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A signature's placeholders, in this order: `<str>` a double-quoted string (backslash escapes included); `<uuid>`; `<time>` an ISO date, a date and time (`T` or a space, a fraction, a zone) or a bare `HH:MM:SS`; `<id>` 8 or more ASCII letters and digits, all hexadecimal, with a digit and a letter; `<n>` a number (`0x…` or decimals, with a sign) not preceded by a letter, a digit, `_` or `.`. Single-quoted text is kept, its numbers replaced | Replacing every run of digits; replacing single-quoted text | A bare `HH:MM` is a script location (`script.ts:12:34`), not a time. Names such as `v8`, `utf8`, `sha256` and `x86_64` stay, `5000ms` becomes `<n>ms`. A run of decimal digits alone is a number, not an id. |
| A history that cannot be folded past an entry gives the snapshot of the entries before it, with a warning | Failing the feed item | The item would be applied again forever; the journal stays the record. |
| A projection of a run that is quarantined, or has ended, also records the quarantine its record holds: a quarantine as of its start, none as of the run's last commit | Only whoever changes it records it | A server that died between quarantining a run and recording it would leave the read model wrong, and a run that ends quarantined is released by its last commit, which nobody else records; any later record by whoever changed it still wins. |
| `run_tool_task` returns the state it leaves the task in when the task waits for the harness (parked, deferred), which the dispatcher records; the projector reads a run's tasks only after a commit that scheduled calls, records quarantine only for a run quarantined or ended, and reads each script's headline and toolset once per batch | Reading the run's tasks after every call; recording tasks and quarantine at every projection | The first version did, and the load of 1,000 runs went from an activation p99 under 1 s to 4.8 s, and from 19 s to 50 s of lag in the read model: four more round trips per projected item and two per call, with the dispatcher's slot held. A task is created waiting for an approval by the commit that schedules it; every later change has an owner that records it. |
| `GET /v1/runs` overlays each listed run's status, quarantine and times from the run's own record, and drops its waits once that record has ended | The read model alone | The projector follows commits by a moment: a run that just ended is shown ended. Filters still read the read model. |
| A page's cursor is the run id for `sort=started`, and the sort key in milliseconds (`-` without one), a dot and the run id otherwise; a series and a failure group have 500 buckets at most | An opaque encoded cursor | The `started` cursor stays the one clients already pass. A bucket of 1 ms over a month would allocate millions of buckets. |
| The Turso adapter counts statistics, facets and failure groups in Rust over the runs a query matches, by the in-memory adapter's rules; what a run waits for is computed in SQL from `run_waits` joined to the calls' task states | SQL aggregates in Turso | The embedded server holds one machine's runs; the rules stay those of the reference adapter. |
| Task states and quarantine are kept by run id alone, so they can be recorded before the run is projected; `delete_run` removes them even for a run never projected | Dropping records of a run not projected yet | A claim can be recorded before the projector applies the commit that scheduled the call. |
| Postgres takes a run's summary row `FOR UPDATE` (or inserts a placeholder) before deciding whether a snapshot is longer, and replaces the waits under that lock | A conditional upsert alone | Two projectors may apply items of one run at once; the waits, labels and text must follow the snapshot that won. A test applies 12 runs from 3 stores at once, in different orders. |
| `search_text` lives in a table of its own (`run_search`), written when it changes | A column of `run_summaries` | Every batch rewrites the summary row; with `last_activity_at` indexed, the update is not in place and would insert every trigram again. |
| Every subquery of a Postgres query repeats the tenant, label and tool vocabularies have extended statistics (MCV), id and digest prefixes become `uuid` and `bytea` ranges, and every text compared or ordered uses the "C" collation | Letting the planner guess | On 200k runs and 1M calls, a rare label went from a sequential scan (80 ms) to an index scan (0.2 ms), and paging a frequent label from a hash of every run (73 ms) to an ordered read (0.5 ms). "C" gives byte order, which the contract's tie-breaks are. |
| Postgres answers statistics, failure groups, scripts and tool statistics each in one SQL statement over indexed sets: the window's runs by start, waiting runs from a partial index on active runs, quarantined runs from their own table; percentiles with `percentile_disc` | Loading rows into Rust | One consistent snapshot per answer, without moving the tenant's rows. A test checks that `percentile_disc` is the nearest rank for up to 500 values at p50, p95 and p99. |

### Taken while building the dashboard's namespaces (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The run views read through a client bound to the namespace of the filter, which sends `Rewind-Namespace` with each of their requests, and their cache is keyed by it; the Namespaces views and the filter's suggestions read without the header | The header on every request of the dashboard | A read with the header covers one namespace; the suggestions and the Namespaces view need every end user. What was read for one end user is never shown for another. Superseded by [the redesign](#taken-for-the-dashboards-redesign-november-2026): the dashboard has no namespace filter. |
| The app's `Link`, `NavLink`, `Navigate` and `useNavigate` carry `ns` to wherever they lead, unless the target names one; Biome refuses the router's own elsewhere | Adding `ns` at each link; keeping the filter in memory | The URL stays the filter's only state, and a link that forgot it would widen the views without a word. Superseded by [the redesign](#taken-for-the-dashboards-redesign-november-2026): the dashboard has no namespace filter. |
| The filter suggests the namespaces whose name starts with what is typed, the most active this month first (`order=runs`), eight at most; a valid name the list does not offer is taken as typed | Loading every namespace; searching inside names | A tenant can have many thousands, and `GET /v1/namespaces` takes a prefix; without `namespaces:read` the filter still narrows. Superseded by [the redesign](#taken-for-the-dashboards-redesign-november-2026): the dashboard has no namespace filter. |
| Picking a namespace on a run's page opens the runs of that namespace | Staying on the page | A run belongs to one namespace: under another, it is not found. Superseded by [the redesign](#taken-for-the-dashboards-redesign-november-2026): the dashboard has no namespace filter. |
| Over every namespace, the Runs view's Namespace facet is `RunStats.facets.namespaces`, the runs the query matches; while the statistics load, it counts the runs shown | Counting the runs shown only | The facet counts what the query matches, as the other facets do; the runs shown are a page. |
| A quota's share is floored: orange from 80 %, red at 100 %; a CPU above its quota keeps a full bar and shows its share (`160%`); a limit of 0 shows "blocked" | Rounding the share | A meter never turns orange below the threshold that alerts, and an overrun reads at a glance. |
| Erasing a namespace asks to type its name | The inline "Yes" of the other destructive actions | Erasing an end user takes their runs and cannot be undone. |

### Taken while deploying Rewind on Railway and Vercel (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The hosted deployment is under `rewind.codes` (the owner, 13 November 2026): `rewind.codes` (landing), `app.rewind.codes` (dashboard), `api.rewind.codes` (API) | Keeping the code's names (`ptc`, `PTC_*`, `ptc_sk_`) apart from the deployment's | The owner renamed the whole project to Rewind the same day (Product). |
| The API and the workers run on Railway (`deploy/railway/`): services `api` and `worker` from one image, Railway's Postgres template, a Railway bucket for S3, in `europe-west4`. Railway builds `deploy/railway/Dockerfile`, the server stage without BuildKit cache mounts, from an upload of the working tree | An image pushed to GHCR; the repository's `Dockerfile` | The workspace's plan (Hobby) cannot pull private images, and Railway restricts a cache mount's id to one service. |
| The image on Railway listens on `[::]:8080` | `0.0.0.0:8080` | Railway's private network is IPv6: the edge reaches the api and the workers there. |
| The health check of both services is `GET /`, the embedded dashboard | A health route | It answers without a key, and the frozen API has no health route. |
| The dashboard is also deployed on Vercel, and `/v1/*` is rewritten there to `api.rewind.codes` | CORS on the API; an API URL in the build | The dashboard calls `/v1` on the origin that serves it (`dashboard/README.md`), so it needs no change. Event streams pass through the rewrite (checked); a dropped stream resumes from `from`. |
| The landing is static HTML, CSS and a small script in `landing/`, deployed on Vercel | A site framework | One page, no build. |
| Someone new joins the waitlist on the landing itself: its form posts the email to `/auth/waitlist` on the landing's origin, which `landing/vercel.json` rewrites to `api.rewind.codes`, and the hero's first button leads to it | CORS on the API; sending them to the dashboard's sign-in to join; the hero's first button to the quickstart | The owner asked that joining stays on the landing (16 November 2026). The rewrite is the dashboard's: the API keeps no CORS, and only this route is rewritten, since the landing signs no one in. The quickstart starts by signing in to the dashboard, which someone new can do only once approved. |
| CI deploys the two sites to Vercel's production on every push to `main` (jobs `deploy-dashboard`, after the dashboard's checks, and `deploy-landing`), with `vercel deploy --prod` and a team token in the secret `VERCEL_TOKEN`. The API and the workers stay deployed by hand (`railway up`) | Vercel's Git integration; deploying by hand | The Git integration deploys before CI's checks and a preview for every branch; by hand, `main` and production drift apart (the redesign of 15 November was not on `app.rewind.codes`). A Rust release build takes Railway about 10 minutes for each push, and the services are not connected to the repository. |
| On Railway, gVisor and the egress gateway are absent: V8 and the in-process egress checks of the `http` tool and of MCP servers remain (private, unique-local, carrier-grade NAT and metadata addresses refused, so a script cannot reach `*.railway.internal`). Decided by the owner (13 November 2026): Rewind has one user | Not running workers on Railway | Railway offers no choice of runtime, no network policy and no `NET_ADMIN`; with one tenant, the walls between tenants protect no one. |
| The edge and the api have Railway's serverless setting on; the worker and Postgres do not (the owner, 13 November 2026) | Workers that rest when idle, woken by the api and by a cron | The worker polls the store and holds the timers, retries and deadlines; letting it sleep would change 06 and 07 for a saving of a few dollars a month. |
| An edge in front of the api on Railway, as in the Compose deployment: Caddy behind `api.rewind.codes`, `POST /v1/test` to the workers, the rest to the api, over the private network. It retries a refused connection for 30 s | The api forwarding `/v1/test`; no `/v1/test` | Railway has no path routing, and the api answers `/v1/test` with 503 (08). The retry hides the api's restart after it slept. |
| No `REWIND_OTLP_ENDPOINT` on Railway | Exporting to a collector | The metric reader exports every 60 s, which would keep the api awake. |

### Taken while publishing the SDK on npm (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `@rewind.codes/sdk` is the only package on npm; the dashboard and the acceptance suite stay private, and the `rewind` binary is not distributed through npm | Packages of the binary per platform on npm | The SDK is the harness developer's dependency (Product). The binary carries V8 and tsgo for four platforms: its distribution has no design yet. |
| The SDK has its own SemVer version, independent of the crates', tagged `sdk-v<version>`; before 1.0 a minor version may break its API, a patch does not. `CHANGELOG.md` has a section per version | One version for the whole repository; Changesets | The SDK's contract is its TypeScript API over `/v1`, which changes at its own pace. One package needs no release tool. |
| A pushed tag `sdk-v<version>` publishes through `.github/workflows/release-sdk.yml`: the tag must equal `package.json`'s version, be SemVer without build metadata, sit on `main` and have its changelog section; the checks run, `prepack` builds `dist/` from a clean directory, and a prerelease goes to the `next` dist-tag. npm trusts the workflow (trusted publishing over OIDC, with provenance); a version already on npm is skipped | A token in the repository's secrets; publishing from a laptop | No long-lived secret, and every version is attested to its commit. The first version was published by hand, since npm trusts a workflow only for a package that exists. The trust is set once in the package's settings on npmjs.com: npm asks for two-factor authentication, which a token cannot give. |
| The package ships `src/` beside `dist/`, and its README links to GitHub with absolute URLs | Dropping the source maps | The declaration and source maps point into `src/`: "go to definition" lands in the TypeScript source. npm resolves relative links against the repository's root, not `sdk/typescript`. |

### Taken while building workflows across runs (November 2026)

| Decision | Instead of | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A pinned workflow gives two built-ins, `workflows.<name>.start` and `workflows.<name>.signal`, routed `Builtin::Workflow { workflow, op }` and derived from the workflow when the toolset is built; they are not in the document. The namespace `workflows` is reserved: no MCP server or host-tool group takes it, and a toolset may declare no such built-in | A route of their own; built-ins listed in the document | They are tool calls in every way (tasks, keys, forks, mocks, spans, statistics), and the document only needs the workflow: what is derived from it cannot disagree with it. |
| The pinned toolset document has `workflows` only when the toolset pins some; the workflow version document is `{ v, name, description?, script, toolset, schemas: { input, output, signals } }`, canonical, and its digest is the version | `workflows: []` always | The digests of the toolsets without workflows, and every recorded journal, stay as they were. |
| The JSON Schema validator moved from the runtime to the engine (`rewind_engine::schema`), with the same messages: the sandbox checks tool args, a start's input and a signal's payload with it, and the engine checks the inputs and signals that come through the API | Two validators | One place decides what a schema accepts and how a refusal reads; the runtime depends on the engine already. Its messages reach scripts, so it stays part of the epoch. |
| The glue passes a start's options to the op as given, and the op refuses those it does not know; `ttl` is a number of milliseconds or a duration. `result()` checks the output against the pinned output schema through an op (`op_rewind_check_workflow_output`) | Options copied by name in the glue | A misspelled option is a `TypeError` at the call, not an option ignored. The schema is pinned, so the check replays. |
| The glue keeps an end that arrives before its start's result, answers `result()` the same each time, and refuses `signal()` of a name starting with `rewind:` with a `TypeError`. `WorkflowError`'s message is `workflow <name> (run <id>) failed: <Name>: <message>`, `was cancelled: …`, `was aborted: <kind>: <message>`, `returned an output its schema refuses: <problems>` | Ends delivered only to a waiting `result()` | A child can end before its parent's activation that records the start: the inbox orders them as they came. |
| The run key of a child is `rewind:` and its call's key, in the tenant's run keys; a signal from a script carries `rewind:` and its call's key; an end carries its own name as key | Keys of their own | An attempt after a lost claim finds the same child or delivers the same signal; a fork's call made live carries the fork's key, and so starts its own child. |
| The engine cancels an attached child created after its caller ended | Leaving it | A cancellation does not wait for a start in flight, so the caller's end can come first and find no child to cancel. |
| A fork watches the runs its copied history started, unless the copy holds their end, when its first activation copies them (`watch_inherited`); a run that already ended has its end enqueued by the engine under the store's key | Children re-parented to the fork | The child stays the parent's: only its starter cancels it, and its record keeps one `started_by`. |
| In mock mode, the mock sources answer a start with the child's outcome; the engine draws the child's id from its entropy and enqueues the end after the start's result. The mock dispatcher accepts a signal no source answers (`{}`). A cassette records a start with the outcome of its child, read from the end in the journal: the output, else a failure `<Name>: <message>`, `cancelled: …` or `aborted: …`; a child that had not ended is a failure that says so; the `rewind:ended:` signals stay out of its timeline | Mocks that return a run id and scenarios that send ends | A scenario says what a child returns, not how its id looks, and a cassette recorded from a real run replays against the same mocks. |
| Registering a workflow checks its script against `rewind-workflow.d.ts` (its `Signals`, which merge into the `rewind` module) and `entry.ts` (`const entry: (input: Input) => Output \| Promise<Output> = main`), both from `rewind-codegen`; a diagnostic of `entry.ts` is reported at the script's default export, prefixed with "the default export does not match the workflow's schemas". `rewind.d.ts` types `signal()` with `SignalName` and `SignalPayload`, which fall back to any name and `T` while `Signals` is empty | A check of the schemas against the script's types in Rust | tsgo says why the types differ, in its words; ad hoc scripts keep `signal<T>(name)` as before. |
| `rewind-tools.d.ts` declares `rewind:workflows` only for a toolset that pins workflows; the checker declares the module empty otherwise | The empty module in every declaration file | The declarations already stored, and the golden tests of codegen, stay as they were. |
| A workflow is named `name`, its latest version among those the caller sees, or `name@<version>`, a digest or its first 12 hex digits at least. Versions are ordered by creation time, then by the order they were stored in. A key limited to labels sees, pins and starts only the workflows carrying them | Numbered versions | Versions are content-addressed like scripts and toolsets; 12 digits are how the rest of Rewind shortens a digest. |
| Problems `invalid-workflow` (a name, a signal's name or a schema), `invalid-input` (a run of a workflow) and `invalid-signal`; a script that does not check is `invalid-script` | One problem for all | A client can tell the model what to fix. |
| `POST /v1/toolsets` takes `base`: the servers and tools of a registered toolset are kept as pinned, without listing its servers again, and its workflows are pinned again or added; the derived toolset takes the base's credentials unless the request gives its own. The agent tools pin the exact version they just saved (`name@<version>`) | Registering the whole toolset again | The agent tools pin a workflow the model just saved without reconnecting to every MCP server, and its runs reach the servers with the same credentials; a version saved meanwhile by someone else is not pinned instead. |
| The stores keep watchers in `run_watchers (run, watcher, seq, attached)`. Postgres reads the runs an end reaches before locking, then locks them with its own run in id order and reads again; Turso orders a name's versions with a `stored_order` column | Locks taken as rows come | Two related runs that end at once took each other's locks in opposite orders: a test with 24 pairs ending together saw 91 deadlocks without the order, none with it. |
| The e2e corpus runs a case `live` when its scenario says so, registers its `workflows` with the scenario's tools as their toolset, and checks the statuses of the runs the case's run started (`children`); it records only the case's run's journal | A journal per run | The case's run's journal holds every start and end; the children's are checked by status. |
| The workflow endpoints take the toolsets' permissions: saving a workflow needs `toolsets:write`, listing workflows and their versions `toolsets:read`; starting a run of a workflow is `POST /v1/runs`, `runs:start`. A workflow saved under a grant limited to labels carries them, as a key's did before grants | Permissions `workflows:read` and `workflows:write` | A workflow is only used through a toolset that pins it: whoever saves one derives a toolset next (`save_workflow` does both), so the two rights always go together, and the built-in roles stay as designed |
| The store migrations of workflows come after access's: Postgres `0003_workflows.sql` and `0004_run_workflows.sql`, Turso's versions 3 and 4 | Renumbering access | Access was on `main` first; a migration, once released, never changes |
### Taken while closing the namespaces (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The workers' retention jobs delete the opaque tokens expired or revoked more than 24 h ago, 1,000 per statement until none is left (`AccessStore::purge_tokens`, every tenant at once); Postgres skips the rows another worker is deleting | Keeping them; deleting a token when a request finds it ended | A token per session grows the table without end, and a request may never come. A revoked token, those of an erased namespace included, keeps no row with the end user's name past a day. The day is a margin for the servers' clocks; an ended token is refused the same way, kept or deleted. |
| `GET /v1/namespaces` answers `total`, the namespaces matching `prefix` and `above` across the pages; `list_namespaces` returns it with the page (`NamespacePage`), counted in the statement that reads the page. A caller bound to a namespace counts its own alone | A separate count; no total | The page already computes every namespace's use to filter and order them. The dashboard shows how many end users there are and how many are at 80 %, rather than "100+". |

### Taken while merging the namespaces and the workflows (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A run of a workflow acts for an end user: the request's namespace when the API starts it (`Rewind-Namespace` required, as for a script), its parent's when a script starts it. It takes that namespace's TTL, script lines and quotas; a quota reached fails the script's start call (`unavailable`), naming the quota and its scope | Children in the tenant's scope only | Alice's agent starts runs for Alice: they count against her quotas and stay out of Bob's sight. A refused start is the script's to handle, as a tool that is unavailable. |
| A script's `workflows.<name>.signal` reaches the runs of its own namespace only, then those its scope's labels allow; any other is answered as invisible | Labels alone | The namespace is the end users' boundary; labels narrow within it. |
| The namespaces' migration comes after the workflows': Postgres migration 5 (`0005_namespaces.sql`), Turso schema 5, whose rebuild of `runs` keeps the workflow columns of schema 3 | Renumbering the workflows' | Neither was released; the workflows reached `main` first. |
| `inherit_toolset_credentials` reads a toolset's own credentials with `resolve` for the nil run and the namespace `*`, which no namespace is named | A `get` on `SecretStore` | `resolve` already gives the run's, then the namespace's, then the toolset's; with neither run nor namespace, the toolset's. Superseded by [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026). |

### Taken for the dashboard's redesign (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The look is Geist's, Vercel's design system: the ten grays with a role each (component backgrounds, borders, high contrast, text), the color scales for states and links only, a material (radius, fill, stroke, shadow) chosen by where a surface sits, Geist Sans and Mono bundled from `@fontsource-variable`, Latin only | Keeping the hand-made palette; Tailwind with Geist's presets | One system, measured on Vercel's dashboard (a 256 px sidebar, 36 px rows, a 56 px header, white cards on `background-200`), and no CSS framework in the build. |
| One sidebar, which a view that needs more than a toolbar turns into its own (Observability, Run, Organization), with a "back" at its head; the organization picker and Find stay at its top | A second column of navigation inside the page | Two sidebars split the screen; the global navigation stays one click away, as on Vercel's Logs and Observability. |
| The views are Overview, Observability (every run, the active ones, the waiting ones, the failures), Run, Scripts, Tools and Namespaces; Organization opens from the member's menu | Runs, Waiting and Failures as three views; Organization in the navigation | Waiting and failing are states of runs: one place looks at and filters them, another analyses one. The organization is the account's, not a view of runs. |
| Run is not an entry of the sidebar: a run opens from Observability, the Overview, a link or Find, and the sidebar shows Observability as current meanwhile | A "Run" entry | A run page needs a run. |
| The "back" of a run returns to the list it was opened from, with its query and filters (every run when it was opened from a link); that of Observability or Organization shows the views without leaving the page | Showing the views from every page, as Vercel does | It is the way back the operator takes with the browser, and the list is where the next run is picked. |
| A filter of Observability and the query are one: checking a value adds its term to the query, unchecking removes it, excluding adds `-field:value`; the query in the URL stays the only state | Filters kept apart from the query | A view is shared, saved and reloaded as its URL, and the query bar always shows what is applied. |
| No namespace is picked in the dashboard: it never sends `Rewind-Namespace`; a run's namespace is a column, a filter and the term `namespace:` | The namespace filter beside the organization picker | In the dashboard an end user is a dimension of the runs, not a context to switch into. Amended by [the polish](#taken-while-polishing-the-dashboard-november-2026): the Scripts list sends the header for its term `namespace:`, on its own requests. |
| `#/observability` (`/active`, `/waiting`, `/failures`) replaces `#/runs`, `#/waiting` and `#/failures`, which redirect there with their query; `?ns=bob` becomes `namespace:bob`; `#/runs/:id` stays, its sections at `/journal`, `/logs`, `/trace`, `/started` | Breaking the old links; moving a run under `/observability` | The CLI, the webhooks and saved links point at runs and searches. Since [the plain URLs](#taken-for-the-dashboards-plain-urls-november-2026), the routes are paths (`/observability`, `/runs/:id`). |
| Menus, popovers, tooltips and dialogs are Base UI's (`@base-ui/react`), unstyled, styled with the tokens; the CSS lives in files, one per part | Radix UI; `<details>` menus; Tailwind | Keyboard, focus and dismissal are handled by a maintained library, with no styling to undo. |
| Times are relative ("5h ago") and show the exact time, in UTC and local, on hover; the theme and the time zone are in the member's menu | A time-zone switch in the top bar | The top bar is gone, and an exact time is wanted only when looked at. |
| The mark is the rewind glyph of the landing, monochrome; green says "completed" only | The green "AP" square | The square predates the name, and a green brand would read as a state. |
| Find (`F`) opens a palette: a run by its id, a view, a saved search | Only the run lists | A run id pasted from a log or a ticket is the shortest way to a run. |

### Taken while polishing the dashboard (November 2026)

From the owner's review of the dashboard on 17 November 2026.

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The debugger's source spans the page; the step and the inspector sit under it, side by side, and stack on a narrow screen. Beside the journal, the logs and the runs started, the inspector stays a column on the right | A column on the right of the source | Asked by the owner: the source is what a debugger reads, and its notes need the width. |
| A card clips what it holds to its corners with `overflow: clip`, and never sits in a container that scrolls; a card that must scroll (the inspector beside a long journal) scrolls itself, its header held | `overflow: hidden` on the cards; a column that scrolls | A card's stroke is a shadow (Geist's material), which an ancestor that scrolls cuts, and a gutter or a hatch at a corner overflowed the radius. `clip` makes no scroll container, so what sticks inside a card still sticks to the page. |
| Scripts, Tools and Namespaces search with the runs' query bar, which reads the part of the language their endpoint understands: Scripts labels and one tool (`GET /v1/scripts?label=&tool=`) and one namespace (below), Tools `toolset:` (sent) and `tool:`, a name and their negations (applied in the browser to the tenant's tools, which the endpoint returns whole), Namespaces the start of a name (`prefix`). A term a list cannot read is underlined in the browser, as the server's are; the parameters of older links are read as the query they mean | `q` on `/v1/scripts` and `/v1/tools/stats`; a picker of a field and a value per list | One search everywhere, asked by the owner, without a change of the API: the endpoints' parameters already say what each list can filter by. `q` on those endpoints would be a contract to design first. |
| After `namespace:`, the query bar suggests the namespaces of the facets (the runs of the query), then those whose name starts with what is typed (`GET /v1/namespaces?prefix=…&order=runs&limit=8`), for whoever holds `namespaces:read` | The facets alone | The facets hold the ten most frequent namespaces of the query; a tenant has thousands of end users, and the one sought is often not among them. |
| The Tools' figures are the Overview's tiles; a group of tools folds under its row, as the namespaces fold under the organization's | A strip of figures of its own | One look for the figures of every view. |
| Tools draw latency on one log axis labelled at the durations people name (10 ms, 1 s, 1 min, 1 h): a dot at p50, a bar to p95, a tick at p99, a line to the slowest call, the axis's gridlines behind | A bar and whiskers on an unlabelled axis | The strip could not be read: its scale was a caption, and a tool whose calls all took as long showed a 3 px mark. |
| Any payload opens large in a dialog, its value read on demand as in the inspector | A wider inspector | A long input or result could not be read in a column. |
| The Scripts' query bar reads `namespace:` (one name, not negated, without `*`), sent as `Rewind-Namespace` with the list's requests alone: the scripts of that end user's runs, counted over them; its links to the runs add the term. A namespace's page links to its scripts as to its runs | A `namespace` parameter of `GET /v1/scripts`; the scripts of every end user only | The header already narrows every read to a namespace, scripts included: no change of the API. A script is written for an end user; who wrote what is read from their page. |
| What changes a setting is written in a dialog over its page: a namespace's limits, the defaults, an erasure, a role, a member's grant, an invitation, a new key. The dialog's form is mounted at each opening, from what is saved; a click outside does not close it, Escape and Cancel do.
