| `Signer::new` refuses a key whose `kid` is not its public key's; the seed is loaded through `aws-lc-rs` as a PKCS#8 v1 document, which `jsonwebtoken` signs with (`EdDSA`) | `ed25519-dalek` | Both are already built; a mismatch would publish one key and sign with another. |
| `rewind_outbound::verify` does in Rust the checks of a receiving server (the key of the `kid` in the JWKS, `iss`, `aud`, `exp` and `iat` with 5 s of leeway, `htm`, `htu`, `body`), and `jwk` writes a public key's JWK | Checks written in each test | One implementation for the tests of `rewind-mcp`, `rewind-openapi` and the JWKS endpoint, and for Rust servers. |
| Registration lists a server whose `auth` is `rewind` with a JWT of the reduced set, signed with the tenant's key, which `SecretStore::signing_key` makes at the first toolset that needs it; a server without a public URL refuses (`toolset-unavailable`): no one could fetch its keys. A server whose `auth` is a secret, and for which `secrets` attaches none, is listed without one, and a refusal says to attach one | Refusing such registrations up front | Some servers list their tools publicly. |
| The CLI pins a toolset file's APIs as the server does, at any address and over `http`; it holds no key, and lists a signed server without a signature, saying so when it is refused | Refusing signed servers in the CLI | `rewind check` and `rewind test` need the declarations, not the calls. |
| `--allow-internal-tools` (`REWIND_ALLOW_INTERNAL_TOOLS`) replaces `--allow-internal-mcp` without an alias, for MCP servers, APIs and OpenAPI documents; the `http` tool keeps the checks without exceptions; a refusal ends with "MCP servers and APIs are not reached there unless the operator allows it". The deployments' variables are renamed (`deploy/compose.yaml`) | Keeping the old name as an alias | 0.1 has one name per setting. |

### Taken while building the SDK's and the dashboard's secrets (November 2026)

The `ts` workstream of [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026):
the SDK's resources and `verifyRewindCall`, the dashboard's Secrets and Signing keys, and the
documentation, against the frozen OpenAPI document and a mocked server.

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `verifyRewindCall` is built on `jose` 6.2.14 (pinned, as the SDK's other dependencies): `jwtVerify` with `algorithms: ["EdDSA"]`, the issuer, the audience, `clockTolerance: 5` and `requiredClaims` `exp`, `iat`, `htm`, `htu`; the keys from `createRemoteJWKSet` with `cooldownDuration: 10 s`, `cacheMaxAge: 300 s` (the endpoint's `max-age`) and a 5 s timeout, one key set per JWKS URL for the process | A JWS check of our own over WebCrypto; fetching the keys at each call | `jose` is maintained, checks EdDSA through WebCrypto on Node 22, and its cooldown is exactly 04's "an unknown `kid` fetched again at most every 10 s". |
| The keys are read from the `issuer` the server gives (trailing `/` dropped), never from the JWT's `iss` | Finding the JWKS from `iss` | A JWT naming another organization cannot make the server fetch, and trust, its keys. |
| `iat` more than 5 s in the future is refused by the SDK itself; `exp` by `jose` | `jose`'s `maxTokenAge` | `maxTokenAge` would fix the JWT's 60 s lifetime in every customer's server; Rewind may change it. |
| `htu` is compared as URLs (WHATWG parsing: scheme and host case, a default port), the fragment dropped, the query kept; `htm` case aside | Byte equality; DPoP's `htu` without its query | Proxies and frameworks normalize the host and the port; Rewind signs the URL it calls, its query included, and a replay to another query must fail. |
| A missing `body` claim requires an empty body; a present one the SHA-256 of the raw bytes, from a clone of a Fetch `Request` | Hashing a re-serialized JSON body; consuming the request's body | JSON re-serialized is not the bytes signed; the handler still reads its body after the check. |
| A failed check throws `RewindSignatureError` with `check` (`token`, `alg`, `kid`, `signature`, `iss`, `aud`, `exp`, `iat`, `htm`, `htu`, `body`, `labels`, `call`); keys that cannot be fetched throw `RewindConnectionError`; a body already parsed into an object, or a relative `url`, is a `TypeError` | One error for every case | A refusal answers 401 and parks the call; an unreachable JWKS answers 503, which Rewind retries; a misuse is a bug the developer must see, not a 401 in production. |
| The answer is `{ namespace, labels, run, tool, idempotencyKey }`, all present; a request without the call's claims fails `call`, unless `session: true`, which answers `null` for it (an MCP server's `initialize` and tool listing); `labels` are checked on calls only | Nullable fields in every answer; a second function | An API's handler gets an end user without a check; an MCP server says it accepts the session's requests, which carry no labels. |
| `jti` is not in the answer | Answering it | 04 fixes the answer; `idempotencyKey` is what a server deduplicates effects on. |
| The SDK's resources: `rewind.secrets` (the organization's), `rewind.namespaces.secrets` (a namespace's, beside `namespaces.tokens`), `rewind.signingKeys` (`list`, `rotate`, `jwks`), the helpers `rewindIssuer` and `rewindJwksUrl`, `isSecretName` checked before sending | `rewind.namespace(x).secrets`; no helpers | The same shape as the tokens: a key writes any end user's; the server answers the issuer and the JWKS URL, the helpers build them where only the public URL and the tenant are known. |
| Puts and deletes of secrets are sent again after a 503, as every `PUT` and `DELETE`; `signingKeys.rotate` never | Retrying the rotation | A rotation sent twice replaces the key twice and retires the one just made. |
| `secret-required` is a `ProblemSlug`, its `attachments` a getter of `RewindApiError` | Parsing `detail` | The harness and the agent tools show which attachment failed and what may be attached, from the problem's data. |
| The dashboard's Secrets is a view of the sidebar, after Namespaces, shown with `secrets:read`; a namespace's secrets a section of its page; Signing keys a part of Organization shown with `secrets:read`, its rotation with `organization:write` | Secrets inside Organization; signing keys in Secrets | 05 lists Secrets as a view and the signing keys in the Organization row; 07 gives the signing keys `secrets:read` and the rotation `organization:write`. |
| One table for both scopes (`views/secrets/SecretList.tsx`): name and reference, labels (the organization's only), who wrote it last and when, reads over 30 days (a sparkline and the total), last read (the run linked, the namespace), and what attaches it, read per row from `GET …/usage` (kept 15 s): the active runs in a card, the workflows linked to Testing | A table without what attaches; one request for every usage | 05 wants the runs and workflows in the list and the list endpoint has none; an organization holds tens of secrets, not thousands. |
| A value is typed in a password field (`autocomplete="new-password"`), one line, sent once, and the field emptied after; a multi-line value (a PEM key) goes through the SDK or `rewind secrets put` from standard input | A text area | A value is never shown on a shared screen nor offered to the browser's password manager; the tokens and keys a secret holds are one line. |
| A new value of a secret keeps its labels in the form; a grant limited to labels starts with its own and sends them merged with what is typed; a namespace's secret is never offered to it | Letting the server refuse | `PUT` replaces the labels; "no one gives more than they hold" is known before asking, and the server still decides. |
| A deletion is a dialog of its own (`FormDialog` with a red Delete), its button disabled until `GET …/usage` answers; it says, by scope, what stops: the active runs and those waiting (their next call parked), the workflows' latest versions with their servers (for a namespace's secret, their runs refused at start), and the reads | `ConfirmButton` with a fixed text | 05: a deletion first shows what it would stop; [the dialogs that confirm irreversible changes](#taken-while-building-the-dashboards-connect-claude-november-2026) name the action in red. |
| A run's details list the secrets it attached by server, each linking to Secrets or to the namespace's page for whoever holds `secrets:read` | Leaving them out | 05's Run row names the attached secrets. |
| `PrincipalName` reads `token:<namespace>` as that namespace's token and `connection:<id>` as a connection | Showing the raw text | Who wrote a secret is a string, a principal or an end user's token (frozen contracts). |
| The mocked API keeps secrets and signing keys per tenant, with the server's label rules, and derives the fixtures' attachments from their scripts' imports (`github` the end user's for triage, the organization's for the digest; `slack`, `crm` the organization's); `usage` reads the active runs and each workflow's latest version | Fixed answers | The views and their tests meet the same rules as against the server, and the numbers agree with the runs shown elsewhere. |
| The dashboard's tests that counted 23 permissions count 25, and the role `reader` gives `secrets:read` | Leaving them failing | The frozen contracts replaced `credentials:write` with the three `secrets:*`. |
| The documentation gets three pages (SDK: Secrets, Signed calls; Dashboard: Secrets) and rewrites every mention of credentials; the guide shows `verifyRewindCall` in a Fetch handler, Express (`express.raw`, `trust proxy`) and a NestJS guard (`rawBody: true`); no screenshot of the new views yet | Folding everything into Toolsets | A harness developer reads how to attach, and a backend developer how to check, on separate pages; screenshots wait for `docs/scripts/screenshots.ts` against a built server. |

### Taken while building the attachments of secrets (November 2026)

The engine-server workstream of [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026):
the checks of attachments, the reading of secrets at dispatch, the endpoints, the agent tools, the
CLI and the e2e corpus ([09](09-roadmap.md)).

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The executor tells what a script imports from `rewind:tools`: `ScriptExecutor::tool_imports`, a provided method answering every name by default, which V8's executor reads from the syntax tree it already parses; `import * as tools`, `export *` and `export * as` import every name. Confirmed by the owner | Requiring an attachment for every server of the toolset whose `auth` takes a secret; a lexical scan in the engine | 04 refuses a start for the servers the script imports, not for those it never touches; the runtime holds the parser, and an executor that cannot tell stays on the safe side. |
| The attachments are checked after the script compiles and before tsgo | Before compiling | Its imports are known only from a script that parses, and tsgo is the costliest check. |
| A refusal is `RefusedAttachments { problems, attachable }`: every problem sorted by server, and one list of what the caller may attach (the organization's secrets carrying its labels with `secrets:use`, then its namespace's), sorted, at most 100; `secret-required` copies the list into each attachment but those of a server taking none, and the message ends with it | A list per server; the server computing it | Any secret may be attached for any server whose `auth` takes one; the engine already reads the `SecretStore` for the check. |
| An organization's secret outside the caller's labels is `unknown`; without `secrets:use`, any organization's secret is `forbidden`, without reading whether it exists | `forbidden` for both | A grant limited to labels does not see the secrets without them, as it does not see their runs (404); `forbidden` says what to change and reveals nothing. |
| A version is saved only if each server its script imports whose `auth` takes a secret has one attached, as a start | Checking only the attachments given | Every run of it would be refused at its start. |
| A run of a version checks only that its `ns/` secrets exist in its namespace; from a script, a missing one fails `workflows.<name>.start` with a `ToolError` (`unavailable`), a store that cannot answer retries the call | Refusing with the problem's text alone | 03 says `start` fails `unavailable`; the end user puts the secret, and the script may start it again. |
| A call to a server whose `auth` takes a secret, in a run that attached none for it, fails `unavailable` without being sent | Parking it `secret_missing` | The start refuses it, so only a broken invariant reaches it; putting a secret could never resume it, and nothing falls back. |
| A signed call on a server without a public URL fails `unavailable` without being sent, and `GET /v1/organization/signing-keys` answers 503 there | An issuer from the request's host | The issuer is where the servers fetch the keys: a server without a public URL publishes none they reach. |
| The engine reads a run's mode once from its history and keeps it, for 10 000 runs at most (forgotten all at once past that) | Reading the history at each authenticated call; storing the mode with the run | A run's mode never changes, and no store changes. |
| A call parked `secret_missing` says which secret, for which server, and where to put it again (`PUT /v1/namespaces/{namespace}/secrets/{name}` or `PUT /v1/secrets/{name}`) | The server alone | The harness and the model read the reason, then act. |
| `secret_usage`'s waiting runs have a call to a server the secret serves that is `ready`, in `approval` or `parked`; its active runs are those not ended. The journal suite checks it, after the other checks of secrets | No conformance check | The stores build it in SQL: one contract for both. |
| A secret written over the embedded server's socket is written by `local` | No author | Its caller is the operator of the tenant `local`, which has no principal. |
| A key limited to labels writes an organization's secret with its labels added (a contradicting one is 403), and may not replace one of that name without them (403); a value above 16 KiB is 413 `payload-too-large` | Replacing it; 400 | It sees and writes only the secrets carrying its labels; the limit is a size. |
| Putting a namespace's secret creates the namespace, as minting a token does, and is refused (409) while it is erased | Leaving the namespace unknown | The erasure of a namespace deletes its secrets: the namespace must exist. |
| A secret's usage lists the runs the caller sees and the workflows carrying its labels; the totals count them all | Every run | A grant limited to labels does not see the other runs. |
| `/organizations/{tenant}/jwks.json` is a route without authentication (the frozen router had it under the API's), and answers an empty set for a tenant without keys, whatever its id | 404 for an unknown tenant | Servers fetch it without credentials; an empty set tells nothing of which tenants exist. |
| A registration's listing reads each organization's secret it attaches as `SecretReader::Registration`: the read is counted, and the last read names no run (the API's `lastRead.run` and `lastRead.namespace` are `null`, the dashboard says "at a toolset's registration", every caller that sees the secret sees it). Decided by the owner | The nil run in the caller's namespace, as first built; not counting it | The read is real, and its day counts. The nil run named a run that does not exist, which the dashboard linked to. |
| `list_secrets` is in both sets: in the SDK's without arguments (the organization's secrets and the namespace of the client), in the MCP set with an optional `namespace`; a list the caller may not read (403) is left out. The port `Api` gains `list_secrets`, `namespace_secrets` and `namespace()`; the SDK's `AgentToolsClient` an optional `secrets` (`list`, `ofNamespace`) | One endpoint answering both | The endpoints of 07 are kept; a token reads its namespace's secrets, never the organization's. |
| `rewind secrets` lists and writes the organization's secrets, or a namespace's with the global `--namespace`; `put` drops one trailing newline of standard input | A `--namespace` of its own; the value as is | `echo $TOKEN \| rewind secrets put github` adds a newline no token holds. |
| A toolset file pinned in the CLI's process that attaches listing secrets is refused, pointing to the server | Reading them from the environment | Only the server reads a secret. |
| The corpus's scenarios name a server's `auth`, the run's `secrets`, the secrets that exist (`put_secrets`), the caller's rights (`attach`), a workflow's `secrets`; steps `delete_secret` and `parked`; expectations `auth` (the value each dispatched call carried) and `keys` | Engine tests only | The blue and red cases show attaching, parking and resuming end to end, and their journals hold no value. |

### Taken while fixing the security review of secrets (November 2026)

An independent review of the integrated feature found three medium and two low defects; each is
fixed, with a test that fails without the fix.

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A path parameter whose segment is `.` or `..` refuses the call, and the built URL must keep the API's origin and stay under its base URL's path | Percent-encoding the dots | The URL's parsing resolves dot segments, `%2e` included: a script gave `..` and sent a branch deletion, with the organization's token, to the repository's own path, outside the operations pinned and their policies. |
| A script starts a version its toolset pins only if the version was saved under its run's scope (the grant's labels): `workflows.<name>.start` fails otherwise | Checking the version's secrets against the run's labels | The API already refused it; from a script, a key limited to `env=dev` started a version saved under `env=prod` and read its output, the run attaching the organization's `env=prod` secret. Runs carry labels a grant never limits (Dax's `goal`), so the scope, not the labels, is the rule. |
| One reading of an OpenAPI document expands its schemas to at most a million values, references inlined; beyond, the registration is refused, naming `components.schemas` and `operations` | Memoizing inlined references | A reference used twice is inlined twice: a document of 1.4 KB took 1.6 GB on the API's process. A budget also bounds a large subschema referred to many times, which memoizing would not. |
| The engine takes the attached secret's value, and its base64 form for HTTP basic, out of whatever a call answers (its result, its failure's message and data, a park's message), for every route, as `[secret]`; secrets shorter than 4 bytes are left | Stripping in each dispatcher; leaving it to the servers | A server that quotes the key it refused, or an echo endpoint, would bring the organization's token into an end user's journal and script. The engine is the one place that knows the value and sees every answer. |
| `secret_usage` takes the caller's labels and counts only the runs carrying them; a secret's last read is left out for a caller limited to labels or to a namespace when it does not see that run | Filtering the listed runs only | The totals and the last read named runs and namespaces outside the caller's labels. |

### Taken while proving secrets and signed calls end to end (November 2026)

The end-to-end proof of [secrets and API toolsets](#taken-for-secrets-and-api-toolsets-november-2026)
over HTTP (`crates/rewind-server/tests/secrets_end_to_end.rs`), and the acceptance suite and the
release gate moved from credentials to attached secrets ([09](09-roadmap.md)).

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A signed API's calls are proven on the embedded server, in the Rust tests and in the gate's CLI area; signed MCP servers on a deployed server, on Turso and on Postgres, and on setup A | A test-only option for a deployed server to call an API over http, or to trust a test's certificate | A deployed server calls APIs over https only, and its client trusts the platform's roots (`rustls-platform-verifier`), which a certificate made by a test is not among; 0.1 has no setting for tests. The embedded server runs the same dispatcher (`rewind-openapi`) and the same signer. |
| The deployed servers of the Rust tests sign as `https://rewind.test`, and their fake servers read the keys at the server's own address; the embedded server's API reads them over its socket | Binding a port before the server to make it its public URL | A server behind a proxy reads its issuer's keys where it reaches them; `rewind_outbound::verify` compares `iss` with the issuer the fake server expects, as `verifyRewindCall` does. |
| The Rust tests search the process's logs (a `tracing` subscriber of the test binary: Rewind's crates at every level, the others from `info`) and what the server exports over OTLP/HTTP to a fake Collector (protobuf bodies searched as bytes), besides every answer of the API about what the scenario left, its problems and its webhooks; each search first proves it saw the runs (their ids in the exports) | A Collector in Docker, as `rewind-otel`'s tests | A value is a UTF-8 string in protobuf; the fake Collector adds no container and holds only its server's exports. A negative check (a fake server that logs its `Authorization`) fails the search. |
| `tracing-subscriber` becomes a dev-dependency of `rewind-server`, with the features `rewind-otel` already builds | Capturing standard error | Tests run in one process, and a subscriber writing to a buffer is what `tracing` offers. |
| The gate's canary is the value of secrets that runs attach, of the organization and of namespaces; the gate also dumps setup A's Postgres (`pg_dump --data-only`) and searches it for the canary as text and as hex | Keeping it as credentials | Credentials no longer exist. The dump proves "encrypted at rest" on the real deployment: a value in a `bytea` column would be hex in the dump. |
| The gate's permission matrix covers every operation of `api/openapi.json`, connections and OAuth included (both came after the gate and had no probe); OAuth's endpoints answer 404 without WorkOS, as `/auth` does; a probe may need a second permission, checked after the first (`POST /v1/toolsets` attaching a listing secret: `toolsets:write`, then `secrets:use`) | Probing the secrets' endpoints only | The matrix's own check refuses an operation without a probe; `secrets:use` has no endpoint of its own. |
| Criterion 8's reader sees Members, Invitations, Roles, Signing keys and Connections | The three sections it listed | The dashboard shows Signing keys with `secrets:read`, which `reader` now gives, and Connections with `members:read` (since connections). |
| The gate's signed MCP check waits 11 s after a rotation before the next end user's session | Clearing the SDK's cache of keys | `verifyRewindCall` reads the keys again for an unknown `kid` at most every 10 s (04): a server sees a new key that late, and the check proves the SDK as it ships. |
| `rewind run` refused for its attachments (`secret-required`) exits 64, as the CLI already answered it, and the gate checks it. Confirmed by the owner | 65 | 65 is a script refused at start (`invalid-script`); a missing `--secret` is the command line's mistake, as `invalid-input` is, and the message names what to attach. |
| Setup D (the lifecycle) takes `--allow-internal-tools 127.0.0.1`: a run attaching a secret waits across a full restart and reads it again; an erased namespace leaves no `secrets` nor `secret_reads` row | A lifecycle without secrets | The restart reopens the data keys with the same master key, and the erasure of 06 deletes a namespace's secrets: both are only seen on a real deployment. |

### Taken for the table of runs (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `GET /v1/runs` gains three orders: `oldest` (the first started first), `duration` (the longest from start to end first) and `calls` (the most tool calls first), beside `started`, `activity` and `wakes`; ties go to the most recently started. The CLI's `--sort` and the SDK take them | A direction for every order (`sort=-duration`); sorting the page in the client | The owner asked to order the table by its columns: a direction on every key doubles the cursors and the indexes for orders nobody reads (the shortest first, the fewest calls). A page sorted in the client orders 50 runs, not the runs the query matches. |
| `duration` is that of the `duration:` term, the ended runs' (`RunSummary::duration`): a run that has not ended (or whose start is not projected yet) comes after every ended run, the most recently started first | Counting a run that goes on up to now | A key that grows with time cannot hold a cursor's place: the next page would skip or repeat runs. |
| A page's cursor keeps the order's key as an integer, `SearchCursor::key: Option<u64>`: milliseconds since the epoch (`activity`, `wakes`), milliseconds (`duration`), a count (`calls`); the API writes it `{key}.{run id}`, `-` without a key, and the run id alone for `started` and `oldest` | A key type per order | One cursor shape for every order, and one encoding in the API. `calls` and `activity` change while a run goes on: a run that moves between two pages may be skipped or listed twice, as with `activity` already. |
| Postgres orders `duration` over an expression index, `(tenant, duration DESC NULLS LAST, run_id DESC)`, the expression written the same in the index and in the query (`read_model/query.rs`, `DURATION`), and `calls` over `(tenant, tool_calls, run_id)` read backwards (migration 11). A page after a cursor of `duration`, like one of `wakes`, filters the index's rows from the start of the tenant: its `OR … IS NULL` is no bound of the index. Turso, which has no index for `wakes` either, sorts the rows it matches (no schema change) | A `duration` column kept by the projector; a `UNION` of the ended runs and the others | The expression needs no migration of the data and no change of the projector; deep pages of an order by duration are rare, and cost what those of `wakes` cost. |
| The dashboard's table of runs orders and filters from its headers: each opens a menu with the orders of its column (`Started`: newest, oldest, latest activity; `Duration`: longest; `Calls`: most calls; `Wakes`: soonest) and its filters, terms of the query as the query bar's and the sidebar's (archi/08 above): the statuses and quarantine, the namespaces, the scripts, a window of start, a duration, a number of calls, failed calls and the tools called, what a run waits for, the errors, the labels. A value is asked for, then excluded, then removed, as in the sidebar; a threshold or a window replaces the previous one. A second pick of the order shown goes back to the newest first. The order is in the URL (`sort`); Observability's "Order" control is gone | Sorting and filtering in the client; a toolbar of filters above the table | The headers are where the operator looks at what to sort or narrow; the query stays the one state, so a header's filter shows in the query bar, is saved with a search and follows the link. |
| A namespace's page and a script's page hold Observability's table forced to their scope (`ScopedRuns.tsx`): the namespace and the month, or the script, as locked chips; the terms the headers add as chips one can remove, in the page's URL (`q`, `sort`; a script's older `?status=` link is read as its query); "Open in Observability" carries the scope, the terms and the order. A label or a script picked in a row filters the table in place | A query bar on those pages; links to Observability for every filter | The table behaves the same everywhere, and narrowing a namespace's runs keeps the page around it. |
| The month going on is written open-ended in a namespace's queries (`started:>=2026-11-01T00:00:00Z`), and a list of runs keeps its rows while the runs of a new key load (`placeholderData`), dimmed | The end of the month's window at now | The window ended at now, so the key of the list changed every minute: the table went back to "Loading" and the page jumped, which read as a flash of the whole dashboard. |

### Taken for the live dashboard (November 2026)

Reviewed by the owner with diagrams. The change loop below is **temporary**: the owner is
moving the whole architecture to events, which will replace how the server learns of a change.
The stream's contract and the dashboard's layers stay.

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The dashboard's state lives in TanStack DB collections (`@tanstack/db`, `@tanstack/react-db`, `@tanstack/query-db-collection`), pinned to exact versions, behind `dashboard/src/state/`. Views, `app/` and `components/` read state through its hooks and call its actions; only `state/` calls the API, through `sources/`; a Biome rule refuses the imports that would bypass it | Zustand stores; TanStack Query called from the views, as before | The owner asked for a store with a real separation: the view reads the state, the state calls the API, so that the event-driven backend changes the sources only. A collection is a copy of server rows filled by a load and then by change messages, which is what the change stream and the events to come send; Zustand loads nothing, and its loading, deduplication, retries and cancellation would be written by hand. TanStack DB is a 1.0 release candidate: pinned, and imported by `state/` only, it could be replaced there. |
| A search is stored as the server's answer: `runs` holds each summary once, by run; a search's hits hold which runs it returned and in which order; a table is a search's hits joined to `runs` by id, kept by `state/` rather than a live query, since a scrolled table may show other rows than the latest hits (D7) | Evaluating the query in the browser | Only the server evaluates `q` (labels, windows of time, tools called, failure groups). A change updates the run everywhere at once; which runs a search holds, and their order, comes from the server a second later. |
| The dashboard follows changes through `GET /v1/changes`, server-sent events read with `fetch`, one per tab; signals, cancellations and retries go through the existing `POST` endpoints | A WebSocket carrying the reads, the signals and the changes | Nothing goes from the tab to the server but requests the API already has; a WebSocket cannot send `Authorization`, so its token would travel in a message and be renewed inside the connection. The API is served over HTTP/2, so a second stream per tab costs no connection. |
| A `run` event holds the run's whole summary, as `GET /v1/runs` lists it, filtered for each stream: an end user's namespace, a grant's labels, a connection's grant. A run its caller cannot open never reaches the tab, not even its id | Only the run's id, the tab reading the run again | Rows update without a request; the filter is the one `GET /v1/runs` applies. |
| No resume cursor: after a drop, the tab reads again what it shows. `resync` when a pass reads its limit (500 runs) | Resuming from the last `changed_at` | What is on screen is read again anyway; a cursor would bind the contract to how the server stamps changes, which the event-driven rework changes. |
| **Temporary.** One loop per api process reads the summaries stamped since its last pass, less 2 s for commits that land late, and drops the `(run, changed_at)` pairs it already saw; it runs every second while a stream is open, sooner when the process's own wake counter moves. Every write to a summary stamps `changed_at` (the projector, task states, quarantine, wake times): Postgres with the database's clock, so every process shares one; Turso and the memory store with the system clock. A deleted run is not announced | `LISTEN/NOTIFY`; a stream per tab reading the store | One read per process, whatever the number of tabs, and no new infrastructure (no `LISTEN/NOTIFY`, above). It does not scale past a few api processes: the event-driven rework replaces it, and the contract stays. The 60 s refresh drops deleted runs; a run's page shows its 404. |
| While the stream is open, what is on screen is read again every 60 s; while it is down, the tab reconnects (1 s, doubling up to 30 s) and reads at its former rates (10 to 30 s) | No refresh while connected | A change the loop misses (a commit later than the overlap, a deletion) is fixed within a minute. |
| Nothing on screen moves on its own. After a view's first answer, no loading state replaces what it shows: refreshes, reconnections and resyncs swap data in place; a changed search keeps the previous rows, dimmed (in `state/`: query collections refuse TanStack Query's `placeholderData`, which did it before). Equal data renders nothing, rows are keyed by run, cells keep their size, a read keeps as many rows as "Load more" loaded | Showing every answer as it comes | The owner: the state must never reset the rendering, and the rendering must never move for no reason. |
| An answer that adds, drops or reorders a table's rows applies at once while the table is scrolled to its top; otherwise it waits behind "N new · Show", in a slot the toolbar always has, until a click, a new search, a return to the page, or an answer that arrives at the top | Always waiting behind "Show"; always applied | The owner's choice: live where the eye already is, still where the operator reads. The slot is always there, so the button's arrival moves no row. |
| The run page sends signals (a run of a workflow lists its declared signals, each payload prefilled from its schema; the server's 422 shows under the payload), cancels a run that has not ended after a confirmation, and retries a failed run; each with `runs:control`, hidden without it. A signal carries one `Idempotency-Key` per opening of its form; a cancellation none, the server taking a second one as the first. Retry comes with the retries' branch: it calls `POST /v1/runs/{id}/retry` through a `state/` action once that branch is merged | Signals only | The owner asked for all three. A signal shows "Signal sent" until its `signal_received` entry arrives on the run's event stream; a cancellation, "Cancelling" until the summary arrives cancelled. |

### Taken while building the live dashboard (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A summary's stamp rises by at least 1 ms per write (`changed_at = max(now, previous + 1)`); the rows that existed before the column are stamped 0 | The clock alone; `last_activity_at` as the first stamp | Two writes in one millisecond would share a stamp, and the loop would drop the second as seen. `last_activity_at` may hold virtual time, ahead of the clock: the loop's cursor would jump past real changes. |
| Task states and quarantine stamp the summary only when they change what is kept, and only once the run is projected, and after a retried upsert (an attempt that failed may have committed); on Postgres in a second statement after their upsert | Stamping on every record | No change, no event; the projection that follows stamps it otherwise. The second statement takes its locks in `apply`'s order, never the reverse. |
| A pass reads its limit (500) of changes it has not sent, beyond the runs it sent and reads again in the overlap (at most 4 limits of them). One that reads that many sends `resync` to their tenants instead of their `run` events, and the next pass follows at once. What was sent is forgotten only when no pass can read it again (the read's start never moves back): nothing is sent twice. A stamp read 4 s ago (twice the overlap) is a floor no pass reads before: a quiet organization's passes read its latest change only. A pass that reads only runs already sent, beyond those it reads again, goes on after its last stamp (1 ms later if they share one millisecond). The limit is the hidden flag `--changes-per-pass`, so that a test can lower it | A fixed limit; after a full pass, starting at the last stamp read | Runs re-read in the overlap used up the limit: 600 changes in a second gave `resync` every 2 s in a quiet organization, 250 a second at every pass; forgetting what was sent before the overlap sent runs twice. |
| The loop is also woken by the process's own projector when it applies entries or records a task state or a quarantine; after a wake of the counter it waits 100 ms for the projector, and two passes are at least 100 ms apart. A stream holds the embedded server's `Busy`, as a followed run stream does, and gets `resync` when it falls behind the loop. It checks every second the caller's rights as the store holds them now (an end user's token or a connection still authenticates, a key is not revoked, the grant of a key, a member or a connection still gives `runs:read` with the same labels), and closes otherwise; so does a run's event stream. A member's access token already expired when the stream opens (WorkOS takes them 30 s late) is refused with 401. `Rewind-Namespace` narrows a key's or a member's stream, as on `GET /v1/runs` | Waking on the counter only | A commit wakes the loop before its projection exists: waiting for the projector sends the run once, changed. |
| A changed run the store cannot read (a summary that does not decode, a record) is left out of its pass and logged; after 5 passes in a row that fail, every stream gets `resync` and closes. A pass merges its runs with their records 16 at a time, in order | A pass failing on its first bad row; one merge after the other | One bad row silenced every stream of the process while the tabs showed live; a store that is down sends the tabs back to their former rates and their reconnections. |
| The tab opens its stream without reading what it shows (it has just read it); it reads again on a reopening and on `resync`. A `run` event updates the runs the tab shows or whose page is open; the others wait for the next read of a search | Reading again at every opening; keeping every run announced | The first read is already fresh; a tab open for days would otherwise hold every run of the organization. |
| A search is read again 1 s after the last change, and at most 5 s after the first change of a burst; the scripts' and a namespace's figures count as aggregates (at most every 5 s); the workflows, saved by agents and not by runs, are read on the timer only | Reading only after a quiet second | A steady stream of changes would never leave a quiet second. |
| "Load more" reads again as many rows as are shown and a page more, one answer up to 500 rows, then page after page | Following the cursor from the last row | Every row shown is read again in one answer, so the rows shown and the new tail come from one moment of the server. |
| D7 holds the tables of scripts and of namespaces as it holds the tables of runs: their order is a figure that changes (runs, CPU of the month). A list without a toolbar (a namespace's ten scripts, the Testing sidebar's workflows) applies its answers at once | The tables of runs only | The owner's rule is the screen's, not the runs'. |
| The organization's, the connections' and the namespaces' writes show at once where their outcome is known (TanStack DB optimistic transactions): kept until what they touched is read again, undone with the server's message on refusal; a dialog waits for the server's answer. Creations (an invitation sent, a key) show with the server's answer | Every write waiting for the server | The page answers the operator at once; what the server refuses comes back with its reason. |
| The tab's reconnections wait 1 s, doubling up to 30 s, each wait spread by ±25 %; the waits start over only once a stream has lived 5 s | Starting over as soon as the response arrives; exact waits | A stream the server closes as soon as it opens would otherwise be reopened every second, reloading what is on screen each time; after a restart of the server, the tabs do not all come back in the same second. |
| A search's answer does not take back a run the change stream announced after the read began, unless its history is longer. Whether a table is scrolled to its top belongs to the view: a new search learns it from the view | Comparing the history's length only; the flag kept by each search | A quarantine or a task's state changes a summary without lengthening its history. The table hears of its scroll only when it crosses its top: a new search would otherwise believe it is at the top and move the rows. |
| A run's page is mounted again for another run | Keeping the previous run's details, dimmed, until the new one answers | "The previous rows, dimmed" is for a search the operator changed; another run is another page. |
| The connection's dot sits beside the organization picker at the head of the sidebar | A top bar | The dashboard has none; the sidebar's head is the one place every page shows. |

### Taken while writing the object-storage spec (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Every run-to-run effect (a workflow run started, a signal between runs, an end delivered to watchers, a cancellation of attached runs, a fork) is written in the batch of the fact that caused it, with no message between owners | An outbox between partitions | A run reaches only runs of its own namespace, and a namespace lives in one partition. The outbox carries only what leaves the partition: spans, webhooks, `quota.alert`. |
| Counters are exact in each partition, written in the batch of what they count; owners send the organization actor absolute values after each batch that moved them | Deltas sent to the actor; counters kept by the actor | A count commits with its facts, so it cannot drift; a lost message is corrected by the next one, and a new actor asks every owner once. |
| Executors can be remote: an owner sends an activation to a node whose build supports the run's epoch when its own does not; a tenant's own pool works the same way (`--pool`). An owner keeps sending a run's activations to the same remote node while it lives | Owners that only run the epochs of their build | V8 and ICU cannot coexist in one process, and the owner of a partition must stay its only writer. The same node keeps the instance warm. |
| Every node runs under gVisor | Sandboxing only the processes that run V8 | Every process may run V8. |
| Recovery counts one more started activation for each runnable run; the ready queue takes a tenant's runs with the fewest started activations first, then the oldest trigger | Writing a marker when an activation starts | A start is not a state change worth a write. A run that kills its nodes reaches 5 and is quarantined, while the runs behind it, taken first, commit and start from 0 again. |
| A failed activation is tried again by a 30 s timer written with the failure | Trying again at once | The expired lease used to give that pause. |
| Sending a call or handing out a task is not written; the task stays `ready` in the records while in flight. At recovery, every `ready` task counts as interrupted: sent again with attempt + 1 under `retry`, failed `outcome_unknown` under `at_most_once` | Writing each hand-out | W2. The cost: an `at_most_once` task that waited for a slot fails when its node dies, since recovery cannot know it was never sent. |
| A task handed out again after the grace writes attempt + 1 before it is handed | Counting at the next outcome | The new token and the attempt must agree. |
| The state `claimed` keeps its name in the API: it means in flight, in the owner's memory | Renaming it | `rewind-api` exposes `TaskState::Claimed`. |
| The grace after an SDK stream closes is the policy's heartbeat timeout, 60 s by default; the pinned toolset's field keeps its name, `heartbeat_timeout_ms` | A new field; a fixed 30 s | The stored format needs no migration, and each tool keeps its own value. |
| The concurrency of an MCP server or an API is counted across the organization | Per worker | There are no workers; a limit per organization protects the customer's server. |
| A task's token and a webhook token name their run | Looking tokens up in a global table | Any process routes them to the run's owner. |
| One retention for an ended run, 1 year by default, as a timer written at seal; it erases the run's objects and records | 30 days in the database then 1 year archived; lifecycle rules on R2 | One place erases a run, whatever the tenant's retention. |
| A run's summary stays in its partition's index until the run is erased | A TTL on the index | Erasure removes it, with the rest. |
| The activation's span id is `H(run, "activation", index of its first entry)`, from leaving the ready queue to its batch being durable | `H(run, fence)` | There is no fence; the first entry's index is unique per committed activation. |
| A search reads at most 10,000 summaries per partition per page, and answers a short page with a cursor when it stops | No budget | A query whose terms match few runs must not scan a partition whole. |
| A query goes to one partition when its scope is one namespace (a token, the header, a `namespace:` term of one value), and to every partition otherwise; the edge merges by order | Always asking every partition | Most of an end user's queries stay in one place. |
| Statistics take a window rounded out to whole UTC hours; runs waiting by kind come from an exact gauge; failure groups keep per fingerprint and hour the count, the scripts and lines, and their latest runs; scripts keep runs by status and the tools they called; tool statistics are per tool, filtered by a name prefix, without a per-toolset breakdown; observed shapes are kept per tenant, namespace and tool and merged at the edge | Statistics on any query | E8: a fixed set, chosen now. |
| A grant or a token limited to labels gets 403 on every statistics endpoint | Statistics filtered by labels | Counters carry no labels. |
| The dashboard reads a search again 1 s after the last change of a run (at most 5 s after the first of a burst) and its statistics at most every 10 s; after a reconnection, what is on screen once | Reading on a timer | E5. |
| A webhook's outbox entry stays until its delivery succeeds or is given up; each retry is a timer | Retrying from the sender's memory | A delivery survives a takeover. |
| The control plane's daily jobs: tokens and connections to delete, tests no version references | Doing them at each request | They read nothing on a timer but once a day: scheduling. |
| `ensure_namespace` picks the partition `hash(tenant, name) mod partitions` | The least loaded partition | Deterministic, and the namespace's row keeps it anyway. |
| The node link is HTTP/2 with JSON, on its own port, authenticated by a secret of the deployment (`--cluster-secret`) | gRPC; mTLS | The stack the server already has; Railway's private network carries it. |
| The migration: the new build's Postgres migration moves the run tables into a schema `legacy`; `rewind server migrate-runs` writes the ended runs to the archive with their summaries and retention timers, the live ones into their partitions, their payloads into their runs, then drops `legacy` | A migration inside the server's start | A one-shot command is run once, watched, and can be run again: each run it moved is skipped. |
| The embedded control plane is `.rewind/control.db` | Keeping `journal.db` | It holds the control plane only. |

### Taken while freezing the run plane's contracts in code (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A run id is a UUID of version 8, laid out as a version 7, with the partition in the 16 bits after the variant; `RunId::partition` is `None` for an id of another version, and `legacy_runs` maps the version 7 ids that `migrate-runs` moved | Version 7 with the partition bits; new ids for migrated runs | The bits of an old id are random, so they would name a partition that is not its namespace's; the harnesses keep the ids they were given. |
| A namespace's partition is the first 8 bytes, big-endian, of SHA-256(`{tenant}/{name}`), modulo the partition count; the home partition of a tenant hashes its id the same way | Any other hash | Stable across builds and languages. |
| `CatalogStore` has its own error type, `CatalogError` | `StoreError` | `Fenced`, `RunNotFound` and `Archived` mean nothing in the catalog. |
| The store owns a run head's `history_len` and `sealed`; the engine writes the rest of the head as it is | The store deriving the head from the entries | The owner is the only writer; the store checks what a single writer may get wrong. |
| A run's summary is written with its creation, `pending`; a pending run is listed and searched | A summary from the first activation | A run waiting for its first activation is a run. |
| `EraseNamespace` drops a namespace's counters, shapes and start keys once the partition holds none of its runs; hourly counters otherwise never expire, and erasing a run does not take back what it counted | Counters erased with each run | A tenant's totals keep what its end users consumed; an erased end user leaves no trace. |
| A timer is identified by its kind and run (or outbox entry): setting it again moves it, cancelling one not set changes nothing; a terminal activation clears the run's timers but its retention | Timers by id | One timer of each kind per run is all an owner needs. |
| Counters are kept per hour; a day and a month are windows of hours. A latency sketch has 16 buckets per power of two, about 6 % of error | Counters per day; exact percentiles | One shape answers every window. |
| The owner-based blob methods are `put_in`, `get_in`, `delete_in`, `copy` and `delete_run`, beside the tenant-keyed ones until the run plane replaces their callers | Overloading the names | Rust has no overloading. |

### Taken while building the partitions on SlateDB (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The partition records are sixteen records of `rewind-format` (`rewind_format::partition`): run head, history entry (the event in the stored format of events), inbox item, signal key, tool task, timer, payload, watcher, webhook token, run summary, start key, the counters of a namespace and hour, the shape of a tool's results, the gauges of a namespace, outbox entry, organization state; each `{"v":1,…}` with its fields in their declared order, optional fields and empty collections left out, decoded strictly, pinned by golden fixtures | One record per table of the previous stores | `rewind-format` knows only `rewind-core`, so it defines the records with `rewind-core`'s types and its own enumerations, and `rewind-store-slatedb` converts the engine's types |
| The gauges of a namespace are a record, moved in the batch of every change to a run's head or summary by what the run held before and holds after | Counting the runs of the scope at each read | `gauges` is one read; the owner reports them after each batch |
| A run's records are keyed by tenant and run; its head by its run id alone, so a creation sees an id taken by another tenant | A head lookup before every read | A read of another tenant's run finds nothing without a second read |
| The run index keeps, for the tenant and for each namespace, one family per order (`started` reads `oldest` backwards), each entry holding a copy of the summary | Entries pointing to the summary | A search page is one range scan; the cost, eleven copies of a summary per write, is shrunk by lz4 |
| The hourly counters are written twice, by namespace then hour and by hour then namespace | One order and a filtered scan | The window of a namespace and the window of a tenant are each one range scan |
| The heads of the runs not ended, the heads of the ended attempts not sealed and the namespaces with hourly counters have their own keys | Scanning every head at recovery | Recovery reads what is live, not every run kept for a year |
| Reads see what is durable (`DurabilityLevel::Remote`); a commit is one SlateDB transaction (snapshot isolation), one at a time per log, written as one write batch, then the WAL flushed; a flush that fails other than fenced leaves the log refusing commits | Reading the memtable; writing before checking | No read sees a batch that may not survive, and a refused change writes nothing |
| Within one store, a later open fences the earlier log at once; across processes, SlateDB's writer epoch does at the earlier writer's next write | The epoch alone | An owner moved within a process stops before it writes |
| SlateDB's timers: no WAL flush timer (the committer flushes), the writer's manifest poll 5 min (under the compactor's 15 min checkpoint), the compactor's poll 1 min, its commit of finished compactions 5 s, the worker's poll 1 min, the collector every 10 min; fence objects never collected, no detach task | SlateDB's defaults (1 s, 5 s, 1 s, 5 s, 10 min) | As long as SlateDB stays correct (principle 11); measured by `examples/bench.rs` |
| A level-0 table every 4096 batches or 64 MiB: a takeover replays at most 4096 WAL objects | Fewer | SlateDB refuses less than 4096 |
| One block cache per process, on moka, 256 MiB of blocks and 64 MiB of indexes and filters, shared by the partitions it opens | SlateDB's default of 512 MiB and 128 MiB per database | 64 partitions per node |
| Scans keep their blocks in the cache and read ahead 256 KiB with two fetches; tables and WAL objects are compressed with lz4 | One block per request, uncompressed | R2 charges by request: a cold history is one or two requests |

### Taken while building the control plane of the run plane (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Catalog, access and secrets changes go on `NOTIFY control_plane`, the partition map on `partition_map`, both in the write's transaction; a payload names a kind and keys (`{"k":"secret","t":…,"ns":…,"n":…}`), never a value, but for the partition map, sent whole and compact; a payload above 7,900 bytes goes as numbered frames; a stream that reads a broken sequence ends, and its listener drops its caches | One channel; values in the payload | `NOTIFY` holds 8,000 bytes. The suite wants every notification to carry the map as it committed. |
| A node's lock key is the first 8 bytes of SHA-256(`rewind node/<id>`); the node holds it exclusively on a connection of its own, a watcher waits on it in shared mode on another; the dedicated connections set `tcp_keepalives_idle=5`, `interval=1`, `count=5` and `tcp_user_timeout=10000` | A lease row | About 10 s for a machine that vanishes; at once for a process that dies (measured: 12 ms for a killed process). |
| The locks are taken in a database of their own on the same server (`--cluster-database`, `postgres` by default) | The control plane's database | A backend blocked on a lock holds a snapshot, which holds back VACUUM for its database for as long as it waits. In a database that stores nothing, that costs nothing. |
| The ring is the rows of `nodes`, the dead ones included until the watcher that names a dead node deletes its row while it holds its lock; `leave` only releases the lock | A ring of live members | A ring of live members loses the node that died just before a watch began. |
| A write that changes nothing sends nothing; usage stamps (a connection's last use, a secret's reads, OAuth clients and codes, signing keys) send nothing | Sending on every write | No change, no message. |
| `namespaces.partition` is set for the rows created before it by `place_namespaces`, run once before the catalog serves | A migration computing the hash in SQL | The hash is the engine's, in Rust. |
| The Turso cluster's members live in the process, and its partition map and epochs in the database | A cluster of one member only | The cluster suite joins three members; embedded, there is one. |
| The archive's cache keeps an attempt when it reads one, never when it puts it | Caching at put | Another process may have written the first put, which is the one kept. |

### Taken while building the actors of the run plane (November 2026)

| Decision | Alternative rejected | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Actors never read a store: their reads are jobs whose results come back as messages; the owner keeps the runs it touches in memory and loads one on demand | Actors awaiting their reads | A state machine with no I/O can be stepped one message at a time by the driver. |
| One tokio task drives every actor of a node; store jobs, remote requests and activations run in tasks of their own | One task per actor | The node's task waits on its channel and on the wheel's earliest timer, nothing else. |
| Inbox and outbox ids come from a counter per partition, rebuilt at recovery from the highest id | Ids drawn at random | Ordered and unique within the partition. |
| The edge checks the script, the TTL, the input, the attachments and the namespace, and draws the run's id and seed; the owner applies the start key, asks the organization actor to admit the start, and creates the run | Every check at the owner | The owner's mailbox only does what needs its state. |
| Recovery counts as interrupted every `ready` task that is due, host tasks included: attempt + 1 and a new token; an answer carrying the old token is refused as gone | Keeping the old token | Recovery cannot know whether the task was handed out. |
| Tokens are `rewind_tk_{run}_{hex}` for tasks and `rewind_wh_{run}_{hex}` for webhooks, drawn from a CSPRNG; the owner keeps their SHA-256 | Opaque tokens looked up in a table | They route to the run's owner from any process. |
| A run is quarantined when its started activations without an end reach 5, as with failures | Above 5 | One threshold for both counts. |
| Admissions carry the owner's partition epoch; a report from a newer epoch releases them; a restarted organization actor takes the admissions it saved as epoch 0; a slot asked for again is granted again | Admissions without an epoch | A stale owner's admissions would otherwise stay held. |
| `Change::Summary` writes a run's summary alone, for the task phases (parked, deferred, approved) outside activations; parked calls are found through the index when a secret is put again | Recovery returning parked tasks | A secret put again must find every call parked for it. |
| The driver runs store work first, then activations, then calls ordered by run id; it opens partitions with a horizon of 100 years and jumps the clock only to timers that wake something by themselves | Any order | The e2e corpus records the same journal byte for byte as the recorded one. |
| A deployment with one partition routes any run id to partition 0 | Refusing a version 7 id without `legacy_runs` | The embedded server and the corpus's recorded journals hold version 7 ids. |
